\mathbf{K}_{5/1}\simeq\mathbf{K}_{5}&\mathbf{K}_{5/2}\simeq\mathbf{C}_{5}&\mathbf{K}_{7/2}\end{array}

Figure 1.
Examples of circular cliques.

The fact that circular cliques
\mathbf{K}_{p/q}
with
2<p/q<4
satisfy the topological condition of Theorem
1.4
is folklore, though we prove it later for completeness.
The theorem in this case can be viewed as
NP
-hardness of colouring
(2+\varepsilon)
-colourable graphs with
4-\varepsilon
colours:

Corollary 1.5
.

\PCSP(\mathbf{K}_{p/q},\mathbf{K}_{p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}})
is
NP
-hard for all
2<p/q\leq p^{\prime}/q^{\prime}<4
.

A graph is said to be
square-free
if it does not contain the 4-cycle
\mathbf{C}_{4}
as a subgraph. This includes all graphs of girth at least
5
and thus graphs of arbitrarily high chromatic number.
Again, it will be a simple observation that square-free graphs satisfy the condition of Theorem
1.4
.
Therefore, we confirm Conjecture
1.2
for square-free graphs
\mathbf{G}
.

Corollary 1.6
.

\PCSP(\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G})
is
NP
-hard for all non-bipartite loopless graphs
\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G}
such that
\mathbf{H}\rightarrow\mathbf{G}
and
\mathbf{G}
is square-free.

Since the key assumption of Theorem
1.4
is topological, this raises a question whether topology is in some sense necessary to settle Conjecture
1.2
. Using adjointness, we argue that it is indeed the case, proving the following (see Theorem
4.49
for a formal statement).

Theorem 1.7

(informal)
.

For any graph
\mathbf{G}
, the property that
\PCSP(\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G})
is

NP
-hard for all non-bipartite
\mathbf{G}
-colourable graphs
\mathbf{H}
depends
only on the topology (and
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action) of
\lvert\mathord{\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathbf{G})}\rvert
.

Returning to the right half of the chain (
1.1
) (the classical colouring problem), we first show that,
to prove
NP
-hardness of
c
-colouring
k
-colourable graphs for all constants
c\geq k\geq 3
,
it is enough to prove it for
any fixed

k
(and all
c\geq k
).

Theorem 1.8
.

Suppose there is an integer
k
such that
\PCSP(\mathbf{K}_{k},\mathbf{K}_{c})
is
NP
-hard for all
c\geq k
.
Then
\PCSP(\mathbf{K}_{3},\mathbf{K}_{c})
is
NP
-hard for all
c\geq 3
.

Following the reasoning in
[
GS20a
]
, the above theorem implies
NP
-hardness of all problems
\PCSP(\mathbf{K}_{k},\mathbf{K}_{c})
with
c\geq k\geq 3
if the
d
-to-1 conjecture of Khot holds for any fixed
d\geq 2
.
(The paper
[
GS20a
]
used an earlier version of Theorem
1.8
with 4 in place of 3).

Furthermore, we strengthen the best known asymptotic hardness: Huang
[
Hua13
]
showed that
\PCSP(\mathbf{K}_{k},\mathbf{K}_{c})
is NP-hard for all sufficiently large
k
and
c=2^{\Omega(k^{1/3})}
. We improve this in two ways, using Huang’s result as a black-box. First, we improve the asymptotics from sub-exponential
c=2^{\Omega(k^{1/3})}
to single-exponential
c={\binom{k}{\lfloor k/2\rfloor}}\in\Theta(2^{k}/\sqrt{k})
. Second, we show the claim holds for
k
starting as low as
4
.

Theorem 1.9
.

For all
k\geq 4
and
c={\binom{k}{\lfloor k/2\rfloor}}-1
,
\PCSP(\mathbf{K}_{k},\mathbf{K}_{c})
is
NP
-hard.

In comparison, the previous best result relevant for all integers
k
was obtained in
[
BBKO21
]
where
NP
-hardness of
\PCSP(\mathbf{K}_{k},\mathbf{K}_{2k-1})
is proved for all
k\geq 3
.
For
k=3,4
we obtain no new results and for
k=5
the two bounds coincide:
{\binom{k}{\lfloor k/2\rfloor}}-1=9=2k-1
.
However, already for
k=6
we improve the bound from
2k-1=11
to
{\binom{k}{\lfloor k/2\rfloor}}-1=19
, and, for larger
k
, the improvement is even more dramatic.

The organisation of the paper

Section
2
briefly describes the algebraic framework of
[
BBKO21
]
: minions (sets of polymorphisms of a PCSP), minion homomorphism (which provide log-space reductions between corresponding problems), and a condition on minions that guarantees
NP
-hardness.
Section
3
details the topological method and its application: Theorem
1.4
.
The bulk of its content is devoted to expounding standard definitions with examples and then proving these definitions behave well when identifying variables of polymorphisms.
Section
4
introduces adjunction in a wider context, in particular relating it to gadget reductions and minion homomorphisms.
Adjoint functions that give reductions for approximate graph colouring are presented in Section
4.3
.
Finally Section
4.4
uses another adjoint function to prove
Theorem
1.7
: that whether a graph
\mathbf{G}
satisfies the Brakensiek-Guruswami conjecture for all
\mathbf{H}
depends only on the topology of
\mathbf{G}
.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Promise graph homomorphism problems

The approximate graph colouring problem and promise graph homomorphism problem are special cases of the PCSP, and we use the theory of PCSPs. However, we will not need the general definitions, so we define everything only for digraphs. For general definitions, see, e.g.
[
BBKO21
]
.

A
digraph

\mathbf{H}
is a pair
\mathbf{H}=(V(H),E(H))
, where
V(H)
is a set of vertices and
E(H)\subseteq\{(u,v)\mid u,v\in V(H)\}
is a set of
(directed) edges. Unless stated otherwise, our digraphs are finite and can have loops. We view undirected graphs as digraphs where each (non-loop) edge is presented in both directions.

Definition 2.1
.

A
homomorphism
from a digraph
\mathbf{H}=(V(H),E(H))
to another digraph
\mathbf{G}=(V(G),E(G))
is a map
h\colon V(H)\to V(G)
such that
(h(u),h(v))\in E(G)
for every
(u,v)\in E(H)
. In this case we write
h\colon\mathbf{H}\to\mathbf{G}
, and simply
\mathbf{H}\to\mathbf{G}
to indicate that a homomorphism exists.

We now define formally the promise (di)graph homomorphism problem.

Definition 2.2
.

Fix two digraphs
\mathbf{H}
and
\mathbf{G}
such that
\mathbf{H}\rightarrow\mathbf{G}
.

•

The
search
variant of
\PCSP(\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G})
is, given an input digraph
\mathbf{I}
that maps homomorphically to
\mathbf{H}
,
find
a homomorphism
h\colon\mathbf{I}\to\mathbf{G}
.

•

The
decision
variant of
\PCSP(\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G})
requires, given an input digraph
\mathbf{I}
such that either
\mathbf{I}\to\mathbf{H}
or
\mathbf{I}\not\to\mathbf{G}
, to output
yes
in the former case, and
no
in the latter case.

We remark that the (decision) problem
\PCSP(\mathbf{H},\mathbf{H})
is nothing else but the constraint satisfaction problem
\CSP(\mathbf{H})
, also known as
\mathbf{H}
-colouring.

There is an obvious reduction from the decision variant of each PCSP to the search variant, but it is not known whether the two variants are equivalent for each PCSP. The hardness results in this paper hold for the decision (and hence also for the search) version of
\PCSP(\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G})
.

It is obvious that if at least one of
\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G}
is undirected and bipartite then the problem can be solved in polynomial time by using an algorithm for 2-colouring. If one of the graphs contains a loop, the problem is trivial. Recall that Brakensiek and Guruswami conjectured (see Conjecture
1.2
) that, for undirected graphs, the problem is
NP
-hard in all the other cases.

All applications in this paper concern undirected graphs, even though some proofs use digraphs.
We remark that, as shown in Theorem F.3 of the arXiv version of
[
BG21a
]

(generalising the corresponding result for CSPs
[
FV98
]
), a complexity classification of all problems
\PCSP(\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G})
for digraphs is equivalent to such a classification for all PCSPs (for arbitrary relational structures).

Two (di)graphs
\mathbf{H}_{1}
and
\mathbf{H}_{2}
are called
homomorphically equivalent
if both
\mathbf{H}_{1}\rightarrow\mathbf{H}_{2}
and

\mathbf{H}_{2}\rightarrow\mathbf{H}_{1}
.
The binary relation
\mathbf{H}_{1}\rightarrow\mathbf{H}_{2}
defines a preorder on the class of all digraphs (or all graphs), called the
homomorphism preorder
. We will use this preorder in Section
4
.

We also define digraph powers, which are essential for the notion of polymorphisms.

Definition 2.3
.

The
n
-th direct (or tensor) power
of a digraph
\mathbf{H}
is the digraph
\mathbf{H}^{n}
whose vertices are all
n
-tuples of vertices of
\mathbf{H}
(i.e.,
V(H^{n})=V(H)^{n}
), and whose edges are defined as follows: we have an edge from
(u_{1},\dots,u_{n})
to
(v_{1},\dots,v_{n})
in
\mathbf{H}^{n}
if and only if
(u_{i},v_{i})
is an edge of
\mathbf{H}
for all
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
.

2.2.
Polymorphisms

We use the notions of polymorphisms
[
AGH17
,
BG21a
]
, minions and minion homomorphisms
[
BKO19
,
BBKO21
]
. We introduce these notions in the special case of digraphs below. General definitions and more insights can be found in
[
BBKO21
,
BKW17
]
.

Definition 2.4
.

An
n
-ary
polymorphism
from a digraph
\mathbf{H}
to a digraph
\mathbf{G}
is a homomorphism from
\mathbf{H}^{n}
to
\mathbf{G}
.
To spell this out, it is a mapping
f\colon V(H)^{n}\to V(G)
such that, for all tuples
(u_{1},v_{1})
, …,
(u_{n},v_{n})
of edges of
\mathbf{H}
, we have

(f(u_{1},\dots,u_{n}),f(v_{1},\dots,v_{n}))\in E(G).

We denote the set of all polymorphisms from
\mathbf{H}
to
\mathbf{G}
by
\Pol(\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G})
.

Example 2.5
.

The
n
-ary polymorphisms from a digraph
\mathbf{H}
to the
k
-clique
\mathbf{K}_{k}
are the
k
-colourings of
\mathbf{H}^{n}
.

The set of all polymorphisms between any two digraphs has a certain algebraic structure, which we now describe.
We denote by
[n]
the set
\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
.

Definition 2.6
.

An
n
-ary function
f\colon A^{n}\to B
is called a
minor
of an
m
-ary function
g\colon A^{m}\to B
if there is a map
\pi\colon[m]\to[n]
such that

f(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=g(x_{\pi(1)},\dots,x_{\pi(m)})

for all
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\in A
. In this case, we write
f=g^{\pi}
.

Alternatively, one can say that
f
is a minor of
g
if it is obtained from
g
by identifying variables, permuting variables, and introducing inessential variables.

Definition 2.7
.

For sets
A,B
, let
\mathscr{O}(A,B)=\{f\colon A^{n}\rightarrow B\mid n\geq 1\}
. A
(function) minion

\mathscr{M}
on a pair of sets
(A,B)
is a non-empty subset of
\mathscr{O}(A,B)
that is closed under taking minors. For fixed
n\geq 1
, let
\mathscr{M}^{(n)}
denote the set of
n
-ary functions from
\mathscr{M}
.

It is easy to see that
\Pol(\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G})
is a minion whenever
\mathbf{H}\rightarrow\mathbf{G}
.

An important notion in our analysis of polymorphisms is that of an essential coordinate.

Definition 2.8
.

A coordinate
i
of a function
f\colon A^{n}\rightarrow B
is called
essential
if
f
depends on it, that is,
if there exist
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}
and
b_{i}
in
A
such that

f(a_{1},\ldots,a_{i-1},a_{i},a_{i+1},\ldots,a_{n})\neq f(a_{1},\ldots,a_{i-1},b_{i},a_{i+1},\ldots,a_{n}).

A coordinate of
f
that is not essential is called
inessential
.

Definition 2.9
.

A minion
\mathscr{M}
is said to have
essential arity at most
k
, if each function
f\in\mathscr{M}
has at most
k
essential variables. It is said to have
bounded essential arity
if it
has essential arity at most
k
for some
k
.

Example 2.10
.

It is well known (see, e.g.,
[
GL74
]
), and not hard to check, that the minion
\Pol(\mathbf{K}_{3},\mathbf{K}_{3})
has essential arity at most 1.
However for any odd
k>3
, the minion
\Pol(\mathbf{C}_{k},\mathbf{K}_{3})
does not have bounded essential arity. Indeed, fix a homomorphism
h\colon\mathbf{C}_{k}\to\mathbf{K}_{3}
such that
h(0)=h(2)=0
and
h(1)=1
and define the following function from
\mathbf{C}_{k}^{n}
to
\mathbf{K}_{3}
:

f(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})=\begin{cases}2&\text{if }x_{1}=\ldots=x_{n}=1,\\
h(x_{1})&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

It is easy to check that
f\in\Pol(\mathbf{C}_{k},\mathbf{K}_{3})
.
By using Definition
2.8
with
a_{1}=\ldots=a_{n}=1
and
b_{i}=0
, one can verify that every coordinate
i
of
f
is essential.

Definition 2.11
.

Let
\mathscr{M}
and
\mathscr{N}
be two minions (not necessarily on the same pairs of sets). A mapping
\xi\colon\mathscr{M}\to\mathscr{N}
is called a
minion homomorphism
if

(1)

it preserves arities, i.e., maps
n
-ary functions to
n
-ary functions for all
n
, and

(2)

it preserves taking minors, i.e., for each
\pi\colon\{1,\dots,m\}\to\{1,\dots,n\}
and each
g\in\mathscr{M}^{(m)}
we have
\xi(g)^{\pi}=\xi(g^{\pi})
, i.e.,

\xi(g)(x_{\pi(1)},\dots,x_{\pi(m)})=\xi(g(x_{\pi(1)},\dots,x_{\pi(m)})).

We refer to
[
BBKO21
, Example 2.22]
for examples of minion homomorphisms.

Our proof of Theorem
1.4
is based on the following result. It is a special case of a result in
[
BBKO21
]
(that generalised
[
AGH17
, Theorem 4.7]
). We remark that the proof of this theorem is by a reduction from Gap Label Cover, which is a common source of inapproximability results.

Theorem 2.12

(
[
BBKO21
, Proposition 5.15]
)
.

Let
\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G}
be digraphs such that
\mathbf{H}\to\mathbf{G}
.
Assume that there exists a minion homomorphism
\xi\colon\Pol(\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G})\rightarrow\mathscr{M}
for some minion
\mathscr{M}
on a pair of (possibly infinite) sets such that
\mathscr{M}
has bounded essential arity and does not contain a constant function (i.e., a function without essential variables). Then
\PCSP(\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G})
is
NP
-hard.

To prove Theorem
1.4
, we will use Theorem
2.12
with the minion
\mathscr{M}=\mathscr{Z}_{\leq N}
, for some constant
N>0
.
The set
\mathscr{Z}_{\leq N}
is defined to consist of all linear functions
f
on
\mathbb{Z}
of the form

f(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{i}x_{i}

for some
c_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{i}
is odd and
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lvert\mathord{c_{i}}\rvert\leq N
.
It easy to see that
\mathscr{Z}_{\leq N}
is indeed a minion and that all functions in it have between 1 and
N
non-zero coefficients, meaning that it has bounded essential arity and contains no constant function.

3.
Topology

All graphs in this section are assumed to be undirected and loopless.

3.1.
Simplicial complexes

An
(abstract) simplicial complex
is a family of non-empty sets
\mathsf{K}
that is downwards closed, i.e., if
\sigma_{1}\in\mathsf{K}
,
\sigma_{2}\neq\emptyset
and
\sigma_{2}\subseteq\sigma_{1}
, then
\sigma_{2}\in\mathsf{K}
. Each
\sigma\in\mathsf{K}
is called a
face
. The elements in these sets are
vertices
of
\mathsf{K}
. We denote the set of all vertices of
\mathsf{K}
by
V(\mathsf{K})
, i.e.,
V(\mathsf{K}):=\bigcup_{\sigma\in\mathsf{K}}\sigma
. A
simplicial map
between complexes
\mathsf{K}
and
\mathsf{K}^{\prime}
is a function
f\colon V(\mathsf{K})\to V(\mathsf{K}^{\prime})
that preserves faces, i.e., if
\sigma\in\mathsf{K}
then
f(\sigma):=\{f(v)\mid v\in\sigma\}\in\mathsf{K}^{\prime}
.
Two simplicial complexes
\mathsf{K}
and
\mathsf{K}^{\prime}
are
isomorphic
if there are simplicial maps
\alpha\colon\mathsf{K}\to\mathsf{K}^{\prime}
and
\beta\colon\mathsf{K}^{\prime}\to\mathsf{K}
such that both
\alpha\beta
and
\beta\alpha
are identity maps.

We will use the following notion of a product of simplicial complexes (see also
[
Mat19
, Section 2.2]
and
[
Koz08
, Definition 4.25]
).

Definition 3.1
.

Let
\mathsf{K}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{K}_{n}
be simplicial complexes. We define the product
\mathsf{K}_{1}\times\dots\times\mathsf{K}_{n}
to be the simplicial complex with vertices

V(\mathsf{K}_{1}\times\dots\times\mathsf{K}_{n})=V(\mathsf{K}_{1})\times\dots\times V(\mathsf{K}_{n}),

so that
\sigma\subseteq V(\mathsf{K}_{1}\times\dots\times\mathsf{K}_{n})
is a face if there are faces
\sigma_{1}\in\mathsf{K}_{1}
, …,
\sigma_{n}\in\mathsf{K}_{n}
such that
\sigma\subseteq\sigma_{1}\times\dots\times\sigma_{n}
.

3.1.1.
From graphs to simplicial complexes.

As mentioned before, there are several ways to assign a simplicial complex to a graph. For our use, the most convenient is the
homomorphism complex
. Our definition of this complex is slightly different from that in
[
BK06
,
Koz08
]
, but the difference is superficial (as we explain in Appendix
A
). The vertices of such a complex are homomorphisms, while faces are determined by multihomomorphisms defined below.

Definition 3.2
.

A
multihomomorphism
from
\mathbf{K}
to
\mathbf{G}
is a mapping
f\colon V(K)\to 2^{V(G)}
such that, for each
(u,v)\in E(K)
, we have
f(u)\times f(v)\subseteq E(G)
.

Definition 3.3
.

Let
\mathbf{K}
and
\mathbf{G}
be two graphs. We define a simplicial complex
\Hom(\mathbf{K},\mathbf{G})
as follows. Its vertices are homomorphisms from
\mathbf{K}
to
\mathbf{G}
, and
\sigma=\{f_{1},\dots,f_{\ell}\}
is a face if the mapping
u\mapsto\{f_{1}(u),\dots,f_{\ell}(u)\}
is a multihomomorphism from
\mathbf{K}
to
\mathbf{G}
.

We work almost exclusively with complexes
\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathord{\mathbf{G}})
, where
\mathbf{K}_{2}
is the two-element clique.
Such complexes (with our definition) appeared before, e.g. in
[
Mat17
]
, where they are called box complexes
(which is not the traditional use of this name) and in
[
MZ04
]
, where these complexes appear under the name
\mathsf{B_{edge}}(\mathbf{G})
.
The complex
\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathord{\mathbf{G}})
can be also described in the following way.
The vertices of
\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathord{\mathbf{G}})
are all (oriented) edges of
\mathbf{G}
.
The faces are directed bipartite subgraphs that can be extended to a complete directed bipartite subgraph of
\mathbf{G}
(with all edges directed from one part to the other); more precisely,
\sigma
is a face if there are
U,V\subseteq V(G)
such that
\sigma\subseteq U\times V\subseteq E(G)
.
The complexes
\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathord{\mathbf{G}})
have an additional structure obtained from the automorphism of
\mathbf{K}_{2}
that switches the two vertices.
The group
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
then acts on the vertices of
\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathord{\mathbf{G}})
by reversing the direction of edges, i.e.,
-(a,b)=(b,a)
.

(0,4)
(0,1)
(2,1)
(2,3)
(4,3)
(4,0)
(1,0)
(1,2)
(3,2)
(3,4)

(0,5)
(0,1)
(2,1)
(2,3)
(4,3)
(4,5)
(5,0)
(1,0)
(1,2)
(3,2)
(3,4)
(5,4)

Figure 2.
Representations of
\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathord{\mathbf{C}_{5}})
and
\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathord{\mathbf{C}_{6}})
.

Example 3.4
.

Let us consider the complex
\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathord{\mathbf{C}_{k}})
. Its vertices are all oriented edges of the
k
-cycle which means pairs of the form
(i,i+1)
and
(i+1,i)
where the addition is considered modulo
k
. It is not hard to see that the only directed complete bipartite subgraphs of
\mathbf{C}_{k}
are either two outgoing edges from a single vertex, or two incoming edges to a single vertex. The only non-trivial faces of
\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathord{\mathbf{C}_{k}})
are therefore of the form
\{(i-1,i),(i+1,i)\}
or
\{(i,i-1),(i,i+1)\}
. The resulting complex can be drawn as a graph (see Fig.
2
for such a drawing of
\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathord{\mathbf{C}_{5}})
). The exact structure depends on the parity of
k
. If
k
is odd, the complex is a single
2k
-cycle where
(i,j)
is opposite to
(j,i)
. The
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action acts as the central reflection. If
k
is even, the complex consists of two disjoint
k
-cycles such that one contains all edges of the form
(2i,2i\pm 1)
and the other all edges of the from
(2i\pm 1,2i)
. The
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action in this case switches the two parts.

Example 3.5
.

A slightly more complicated example is
\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathord{\mathbf{K}_{4}})
. See Fig.
3
for graphical representations of this complex. There are two types of maximal directed complete bipartite subgraphs of
\mathbf{K}_{4}
: either all three in/outgoing edges of a single vertex, or 4 directed edges from a two-element subset of
\mathbf{K}_{4}
to its complement.
These, and all their non-empty subsets, are the faces of
\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathord{\mathbf{K}_{4}})
. In the pictures, the in/outgoing edges correspond to the triangular faces, and the faces containing 4 edges correspond to tetrahedrons that are represented as tetragons. Naturally, all subsets of these tetragons are also faces, nevertheless they are omitted from the picture for better readability. Also note that the outer face of the left diagram forms such a tetrahedron (corresponding to the bipartite subgraph
\{1,3\}\times\{0,2\}
).
The
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-symmetry of this complex is given by reversing edges; this corresponds to the antipodality on the spherical representation.

(3,1)
(0,2)
(1,3)
(2,0)
(0,1)
(2,1)
(2,3)
(0,3)
(3,2)
(3,0)
(1,0)
(1,2)

Figure 3.
Two representation of
\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathord{\mathbf{K}_{4}})
. The tetragons in both pictures represent tetrahedrons with the same vertices.

Definition 3.6
.

A
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-(simplicial) complex
is a simplicial complex
\mathsf{K}
with a simplicial map
{-}\colon\mathsf{K}\to\mathsf{K}
such that
-(-v)=v
for each
v\in V(\mathsf{K})
.
We also write
-\sigma
for the face
\{-v\mid v\in\sigma\}
.
We say that a simplicial map
f
from one
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-complex
\mathsf{K}
to another
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-complex
\mathsf{K}^{\prime}
is a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-simplicial map
, if
f(-v)=-f(v)
for each
v\in V(\mathsf{K})
(note that the first
-
is taken in
\mathsf{K}
, while the second is taken in
\mathsf{K}^{\prime}
).

The map
v\mapsto-v
can be also viewed as an action of the group
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
on
\mathsf{K}
by simplicial maps. We remark that a product
\mathsf{K}_{1}\times\dots\times\mathsf{K}_{n}
of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-complexes is also
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-complex with the action defined component-wise, as
-(v_{1},\dots,v_{n})=(-v_{1},\dots,-v_{n})
.
Every graph homomorphism
f\colon\mathbf{H}\to\mathbf{G}
induces a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-simplicial map
f^{\prime}\colon\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathord{\mathbf{H}})\to\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathord{\mathbf{G}})
defined by
f^{\prime}((a,b))=(f(a),f(b))
.
1
1

1

This is an instance of a more general fact that
f
induces a simplical map
f^{\prime}\colon\Hom(\mathbf{K},\mathbf{H})\to\Hom(\mathbf{K},\mathbf{G})
for each
\mathbf{K}
. This map is defined by
f^{\prime}(g)\colon x\mapsto f(g(x))
.

3.2.
Topological spaces

The spaces assigned to
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-complexes inherit the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetry.

Definition 3.7
.

A
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-space
is a topological space
\mathcal{X}
with a distinguished continuous function
{-}\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{X}
such that
-(-x)=x
for each
x
.
A
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-map
between two
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-spaces
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
is a continuous function
f\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
which preserves the action of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, i.e.,
f(-x)=-f(x)
for each
x\in\mathcal{X}
(note that the first
-
is taken in
\mathcal{X}
, while the second is taken in
\mathcal{Y}
).

As is the case for
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-complexes,
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-spaces are topological spaces with an action of the group
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
by continuous functions.

Example 3.8
.

Prime examples of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-spaces are spheres: We define
\mathcal{S}^{n}
as a subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
consisting of all unit vectors, i.e.,
\mathcal{S}^{n}=\{(x_{1},\dots,x_{n+1})\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\mid x_{1}^{2}+\dots+x_{n+1}^{2}=1\}
,
with
antipodality
as the chosen
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action, i.e.,
-(x_{1},\dots,x_{n+1})=(-x_{1},\dots,-x_{n+1})
.
Other common
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-spaces are toruses. An
n
-torus
\mathcal{T}^{n}
is defined as the
n
-th power
\mathcal{S}^{1}\times\dots\times\mathcal{S}^{1}
, and is therefore naturally equipped with a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action defined to act coordinatewise.

A
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-complex

\mathsf{K}
is
free
if
-\sigma\neq\sigma
for each
\sigma\in\mathsf{K}
(equivalently,
\{-v,v\}\not\in\mathsf{K}
for all vertices
v
of
\mathsf{K}
).
Note that, for a loopless undirected graph
\mathbf{G}
, the complex
\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathord{\mathbf{G}})
is always a free
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-complex.
To ease a technical annoyance in the proofs below, we rephrase the definition of a geometric realisation (see also
[
Koz08
, Definition 2.27]
) of a free
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-complex.

Definition 3.9
.

Let
\mathsf{K}
be a free
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-simplicial complex. Let
v_{1},-v_{1},\dots,v_{n},-v_{n}
be all vertices of
\mathsf{K}
.
We define
\lvert\mathord{\mathsf{K}}\rvert
, a
geometric realisation
of
\mathsf{K}
, as a subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. First, we identify the canonical unit vectors with
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}
, so that
v_{1}=(1,0,\dots,0)
, etc., and
-v_{1},\dots,-v_{n}
with their opposites, so
-v_{1}=(-1,0\dots,0)
, etc.
Second, for each face
\sigma\subseteq V(\mathsf{K})
, we define
\Delta^{\sigma}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
to be the convex hull of
\sigma
, i.e.,

\Delta^{\sigma}=\{\sum_{v\in\sigma}\lambda_{v}v\mid\sum_{v\in\sigma}\lambda_{v}=1,\lambda_{v}\geq 0\}.

Finally, we set

\lvert\mathord{\mathsf{K}}\rvert=\bigcup_{\sigma\in\mathsf{K}}\Delta^{\sigma}=\{\sum_{v\in\sigma}\lambda_{v}v\mid\sigma\in\mathsf{K},\sum_{v\in\sigma}\lambda_{v}=1,\lambda_{v}\geq 0\}.

The action of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
on
\lvert\mathord{\mathsf{K}}\rvert
maps a point
\sum_{v\in\sigma}\lambda_{v}v
to the point
\sum_{{-v}\in{-\sigma}}\lambda_{v}(-v)
which can be equivalently described as reversing the sign of a vector, i.e., as
-(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=(-x_{1},\dots,-x_{n})
.

With the above definition, we can view
V(\mathsf{K})
as a subset of
\lvert\mathord{\mathsf{K}}\rvert
— this will be useful in the technical proofs below. Also note that
-v
has two meanings that result in the same object: either it is a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-counterpart of
v\in V(\mathsf{K})
, or the opposite vector to
v\in\lvert\mathord{\mathsf{K}}\rvert
.
Note that the geometric realisation of a free
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-complex is a
free
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-space
, i.e., a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-space
\mathcal{X}
such that
-x\neq x
for all
x\in\mathcal{X}
.

To express abstractly what it means for two
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-spaces to be the same, we use the notion of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-homeomorphism
which is an analogue of the notion of homeomorphism. We remark that
this is a strong notion of equivalence of topological spaces, akin to isomorphism, and that we will also use weaker notions of topological equivalence (see also Appendix
A
).

Definition 3.10
.

Two
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-topological spaces
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
are
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-homeomorphic
if there are
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-maps
f\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
and
g\colon\mathcal{Y}\to\mathcal{X}
such that
fg
is the identity on
\mathcal{Y}
and
gf
is the identity on
\mathcal{X}
.

Example 3.11
.

It is not hard to see that the geometric representation
\lvert\mathord{\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathord{\mathbf{C}_{k}})}\rvert
of the homomorphism complex of an odd cycle
\mathbf{C}_{k}
is
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-homeomorphic to
\mathcal{S}^{1}
(see Fig.
4
on page
4
). Let us define one such
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-homeomorphism
f\colon\lvert\mathord{\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathord{\mathbf{C}_{k}})}\rvert\to\mathcal{S}^{1}
. Choose
k
points on the circle in a regular pattern. Let us denote these vectors
x_{1},\dots,x_{k}
. Note that since
k
is odd,
-x_{i}\notin\{x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\}
for all
i
. We first define a map
f_{0}\colon\lvert\mathord{\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathord{\mathbf{C}_{k}})}\rvert\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
as follows:

f_{0}(v)=x_{b}-x_{a}

for
v\in V(\mathsf{K})
,
v=(a,b)
, and extend it linearly. Note that the image of
\lvert\mathord{\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathord{\mathbf{C}_{k}})}\rvert
forms a regular
2k
-gon centred in the origin. We project the polygon onto
\mathcal{S}^{1}
by putting
f(x)=f_{0}(x)/\lvert\mathord{f_{0}(x)}\rvert
. It is clear that
f
is continuous and
f(-x)=-f(x)
, and therefore it is a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-map. It is also not hard to see that it is 1-to-1 and therefore invertible, and that the inverse is a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-map.

Remark 3.12
.

While there is always a continuous function between two topological spaces
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
(simply map everything to a single point), there might not be a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-map between two
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-spaces. In particular, the Borsuk-Ulam theorem
[
Bor33
]
(see also
[
Mat03
]
) states that there is no
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-map from a sphere
\mathcal{S}^{m}
to a sphere
\mathcal{S}^{n}
of smaller dimension (i.e., if
m>n
).

Every
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-simplicial map
f\colon\mathsf{K}\to\mathsf{K}^{\prime}
induces a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-map
\lvert\mathord{f}\rvert\colon\lvert\mathord{\mathsf{K}}\rvert\to\lvert\mathord{\mathsf{K}^{\prime}}\rvert
defined as a piece-wise linear extension of
f
:

\lvert\mathord{f}\rvert\colon\sum_{v\in\sigma}\lambda_{v}v\mapsto\sum_{v\in\sigma}\lambda_{v}f(v).

(Here, we use that
v\in V(\mathsf{K})
is also a point in
\lvert\mathord{\mathsf{K}}\rvert
, and therefore a vector in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.)
Consequently, every graph homomorphism
\mathbf{H}\to\mathbf{G}
induces a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-map from
\lvert\mathord{\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathord{\mathbf{H}})}\rvert
to
\lvert\mathord{\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathord{\mathbf{G}})}\rvert
.

3.2.1.
The fundamental group

We briefly recall the definition of the fundamental group assigned to a topological space
\mathcal{X}
, denoted
\pi_{1}(\mathord{\mathcal{X}})
. For more details, see
[
Hat01
, Chapter 1]
.
The elements of the group are
homotopy classes
of maps
f\colon\mathcal{S}^{1}\to\mathcal{X}
defined as follows.
Intuitively, two maps are
homotopic
if one can be continuously transformed into the other.

Definition 3.13
.

We say that two continuous maps
f,g\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
are
homotopic
if there is a continuous map
h\colon\mathcal{X}\times[0,1]\to\mathcal{Y}
such that
h(x,0)=f(x)
and
h(x,1)=g(x)
for each
x\in\mathcal{X}
. Any such map
h
is called a
homotopy
. The
homotopy class of
f\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
is the set of all continuous maps
g\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
that are homotopic to
f
. We denote such a class by
[f]
.
2
2

2

We use notation
[*]
both for sets
\{1,\ldots,n\}
and for homotopy classes; the meaning will always be clear from the context.

Formally, the fundamental group is defined relative to a point
x_{0}\in\mathcal{X}
, but the choice of the point is irrelevant if the space
\mathcal{X}
is path connected (see
[
Hat01
, Proposition 1.5]
), i.e., if any two points in
\mathcal{X}
are connected by a path. Fix one such choice
x_{0}\in\mathcal{X}
. The elements of
\pi_{1}(\mathord{\mathcal{X}})
are all homotopy classes of maps
\ell\colon\mathcal{S}^{1}\to\mathcal{X}
such that
\ell((1,0))=x_{0}
. The group operation is given by so-called
loop composition
: seeing maps
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\colon\mathcal{S}^{1}\to{\mathcal{X}}
as closed walks originating in
x_{0}
, the product
\ell_{1}\cdot\ell_{2}
is the closed walk that follows first
\ell_{1}
and then
\ell_{2}
. While this product is not a group operation as is, it induces a group operation on the homotopy classes defined as
[\ell_{1}]\cdot[\ell_{2}]=[\ell_{1}\cdot\ell_{2}]
(see
[
Hat01
, Proposition 1.3]
for a proof).

Any map
f\colon{\mathcal{X}}\to\mathcal{Y}
induces a group homomorphism
f_{*}\colon\pi_{1}(\mathord{\mathcal{X}})\to\pi_{1}(\mathord{\mathcal{Y}})
defined by
f_{*}([\ell])=[f\ell]
for each
\ell\colon\mathcal{S}^{1}\to\mathcal{X}
.

The fundamental groups of many spaces are described in the literature. For example:

Lemma 3.14

(
[
Hat01
, Theorem 1.7]
)
.

\pi_{1}(\mathord{\mathcal{S}^{1}})
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}
.

We also define
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-homotopy
which is a strengthening of homotopy, restricting it to
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-maps.

Definition 3.15
.

Let
f,g\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
be
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-maps. A homotopy
h
from
f
to
g
is a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-homotopy
if the map
h_{t}\colon x\mapsto h(x,t)
is a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-map for each
t\in[0,1]
. We say that
f
and
g
are
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-homotopic
, if there is a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-homotopy between them.

3.3.
Polymorphisms of complexes, spaces, and groups

A polymorphism from one graph to another is defined as a homomorphism from a power. In the same way, we can define polymorphisms of any objects as long as we have a notion of a homomorphism and of a power.

Definition 3.16
.

(1)

Let
\mathsf{K},\mathsf{K}^{\prime}
be two
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-simplicial complexes. An
n
-ary polymorphism from
\mathsf{K}
to
\mathsf{K}^{\prime}
is a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-simplicial map from the
n
-th power of
\mathsf{K}
to
\mathsf{K}^{\prime}
, i.e.,

f\colon V(\mathsf{K})^{n}\to V(\mathsf{K}^{\prime})
such that
f(-v_{1},\dots,-v_{n})=-f(v_{1},\dots,v_{n})
for all
v_{i}\in V(\mathsf{K})
and

f(\sigma_{1}\times\dots\times\sigma_{n})=\{f(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})\colon a_{i}\in\sigma_{i}\}\text{ is in }\mathsf{K}^{\prime}

for all
\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{n}\in\mathsf{K}
. We denote by
\Pol(\mathsf{K},\mathsf{K}^{\prime})
the set of all polymorphisms from
\mathsf{K}
to
\mathsf{K}^{\prime}
.

(2)

Let
\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}
be two
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-spaces. An
n
-ary polymorphism from
\mathcal{X}
to
\mathcal{Y}
is a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-map from
\mathcal{X}^{n}
to
\mathcal{Y}
, i.e., a continuous map
f\colon\mathcal{X}^{n}\to\mathcal{Y}
such that
f(-x_{1},\dots,-x_{n})=-f(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
for all
x_{i}\in X
. Again,
\Pol(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
denotes the set of all polymorphisms from
\mathcal{X}
to
\mathcal{Y}
.

(3)

Let
\mathbb{H}
,
\mathbb{G}
be two groups. An
n
-ary polymorphism from
\mathbb{H}
to
\mathbb{G}
is a group homomorphism from
\mathbb{H}^{n}
to
\mathbb{G}
, i.e., a mapping
f\colon H^{n}\to G
such that

f(g_{1}\cdot h_{1},\dots,g_{n}\cdot h_{n})=f(g_{1},\dots,g_{n})\cdot f(h_{1},\dots,h_{n})

for all
g_{i},h_{i}\in H
. We denote the set of all polymorphisms from
\mathbb{H}
to
\mathbb{G}
by
\Pol(\mathbb{H},\mathbb{G})
.

In all the cases above, it is easy to see that polymorphisms are closed under taking minors, and therefore
\Pol({-},{-})
is always a minion. This allows us to talk about minion homomorphisms between minions of polymorphisms of different objects (graphs, simplicial complexes, topological spaces, or groups).

Example 3.17
.

By definition,
\Pol(\mathbb{Z},\mathbb{Z})
consists of all group homomorphisms from
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
to
\mathbb{Z}
for all
n>0
.
It is straightforward to check that such an
n
-ary polymorphism in
\Pol(\mathbb{Z},\mathbb{Z})
is a linear function, i.e., of the form
(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{i}x_{i}
for some
c_{1},\dots,c_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}
, and conversely, any such function is a group homomorphism from
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
to
\mathbb{Z}
.

3.4.
Proofs of Theorems
1.3
and
1.4

We recall the statement of Theorem
1.4
.

See
1.4

Theorem
1.3
is a direct corollary of the above and Example
3.11
.
In the rest of the section we prove Theorem
1.4
by using Theorem
2.12
. We show that there is a minion homomorphism from
\Pol(\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G})
to the minion
\mathscr{Z}_{\leq N}
for some
N
. Recall that

\mathscr{Z}_{\leq N}\subset\Pol(\mathbb{Z},\mathbb{Z})
is defined to consist of all linear functions
f
on
\mathbb{Z}
of the form

f(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{i}x_{i}

for some
c_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
\sum_{i=1}^{N}c_{i}
is odd and
\sum_{i=1}^{N}\lvert\mathord{c_{i}}\rvert\leq N
.
This is achieved in two steps.

In the first step, we provide a minion homomorphism from
\Pol(\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G})
to
\Pol(\mathbb{Z},\mathbb{Z})
. This is achieved by following the constructions described above, i.e., the transformations

\text{graph}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits^{\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathord{\ast})}}\text{$\mathbb{Z}_{2}$-complex}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits^{\lvert\mathord{\ast}\rvert}}\text{$\mathbb{Z}_{2}$-space}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits^{\pi_{1}(\mathord{\ast})}}\text{group},

and showing that pushing a polymorphism through this sequence of constructions preserves minors. This essentially follows from the well-known facts that these constructions behave well with respect to products. A detailed proof is presented in Section
3.4.1
.

When we push a polymorphism
f\in\Pol(\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G})
through these constructions, we first obtain a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-simplicial map
f^{\prime}\in\Pol(\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathord{\mathbf{H}}),\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathord{\mathbf{G}}))
, which in turn induces a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-map
\lvert\mathord{f^{\prime}}\rvert\in\Pol(\lvert\mathord{\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathord{\mathbf{H}})}\rvert,\lvert\mathord{\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathord{\mathbf{G}})}\rvert)
. Then, by composing with the assumed
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-map

s\colon\lvert\mathord{\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathord{\mathbf{G}})}\rvert\to\mathcal{S}^{1}

(and assuming without loss of generality that
\mathbf{H}
is an odd cycle), we obtain a polymorphism
g
of
\mathcal{S}^{1}
, and then finally a polymorphism
g_{*}
of the group
\pi_{1}(\mathcal{S}^{1})\simeq\mathbb{Z}
.
As discussed before,
g_{*}
can be described more concretely as a linear function whose coefficients
c_{i}
are the winding numbers of maps
\mathcal{S}^{1}\to\mathcal{S}^{1}
defined by
t\mapsto s\circ g(x_{0},\dots,t,\dots,x_{0})
where
x_{0}\in\mathcal{S}^{1}
is an arbitrary (but fixed) point.

In the second step, we use the discrete structure of the graphs
\mathbf{H}
and
\mathbf{G}
, as well as the action of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, to show that the image of
\Pol(\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G})
under the constructed minion homomorphism is contained in
\mathscr{Z}_{\leq N}
for some
N
. This is described in Section
3.4.2
.

We note that there are several ways to present the proof of Theorem
1.3
.
These presentations would look different on the surface, but in fact they use the same underlying topological concepts, just hidden to various extent. For example, the proof that appeared in the conference version
[
KO19
]
hides topology in a more direct combinatorial approach. Yet another version of the proof can be given in the language of
recolourings
: the required minion homomorphism would map two polymorphisms
f
and
f^{\prime}
from
\Pol(\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G})
to the same function if and only if
f
can be recoloured to
f^{\prime}
by changing one output value at a time, that is, if there is a sequence of polymorphisms
f_{0},\ldots,f_{n}\in\Pol(\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G})
such that
f_{0}=f,f_{n}=f^{\prime}
and, for each
i\in\{0,\dots,n-1\}
, there is a unique tuple
\bar{t}_{i}
with
f_{i}(\bar{t}_{i})\neq f_{i+1}(\bar{t}_{i})
.
However, as shown in
[
Wro20
]
, recolourability is inherently a topological notion, so the resulting proof would have the same essence as the one presented here.
We chose the current presentation because we believe that it reflects the ‘true essence’ of the proof.

3.4.1.
A minion homomorphism

(0,4)
(0,1)
(2,1)
(2,3)
(4,3)
(4,0)
(1,0)
(1,2)
(3,2)
(3,4)

Figure 4.
A representation of
\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathord{\mathbf{C}_{5}})
as
\mathcal{S}^{1}
and
\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathord{\mathbf{C}_{5}^{2}})
as
\mathcal{T}^{2}
.

As mentioned above, the required minion homomorphism is obtained as a composition of three mappings.
The first one is a minion homomorphism from polymorphisms of graphs to polymorphisms of simplicial complexes. We implicitly use that for any graphs
\mathbf{H}_{1}
and
\mathbf{H}_{2}
, there is a natural isomorphism of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-simplicial complexes

\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathbf{H}_{1})\times\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathbf{H}_{2})\simeq\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathbf{H}_{1}\times\mathbf{H}_{2})

given by the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-simplicial map:

((a,b),(a^{\prime},b^{\prime}))\mapsto((a,a^{\prime}),(b,b^{\prime})).

See Fig.
4
for an example.

Lemma 3.18
.

For graphs
\mathbf{H}
,
\mathbf{G}
, the mapping

\mu_{1}\colon\Pol(\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G})\to\Pol(\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathbf{H}),\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathbf{G}))

defined as

\mu_{1}(f)((u_{1},v_{1}),\dots,(u_{n},v_{n})):=(f(u_{1},\dots,u_{n}),f(v_{1},\dots,v_{n}))

is a minion homomorphism.

Proof 3.19
.

Let us first check that
\mu_{1}(f)
is indeed a simplicial map. Assume that
\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{n}
are faces in
\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathbf{H})
, i.e.,
\sigma_{i}
is a subset of edges of some complete directed bipartite subgraph of
\mathbf{H}
. We may assume without loss of generality that
\sigma_{i}
is the set of all edges of a complete directed bipartite subgraph of
\mathbf{H}
which gives
\sigma_{i}=U_{i}\times V_{i}
for some
U_{i},V_{i}\subseteq V(G)
, which form a bipartition of some complete bipartite subgraph of
\mathbf{G}
. By definition,

\mu_{1}(f)(\sigma_{1}\times\dots\times\sigma_{n})=\{(f(u_{1},\dots,u_{n}),f(v_{1},\dots,v_{n})):u_{i}\in U_{i},v_{i}\in V_{i}\}=f(U)\times f(V),

where
U=U_{1}\times\cdots\times U_{n}
and
V=V_{1}\times\cdots\times V_{n}
. By the definition of graph product, all edges between
U
and
V
are present in
\mathbf{H}^{n}
. Consequently,
f(U)\times f(V)
is a complete directed bipartite subgraph of
\mathbf{G}
, since
f
is a polymorphism. This implies that
\mu_{1}(f)(\sigma_{1}\times\dots\times\sigma_{n})
is a face of
\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathbf{G})
.

It is straightforward that the mapping
\mu_{1}
preserves both the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action and taking minors.

The next step is from
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-simplicial complexes to
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-spaces. The map that we construct will not be a minion homomorphism, it will satisfy a weaker condition that will be sufficient later.

Definition 3.20
.

Assume that
\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}
are
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-spaces and let
\mathscr{M}
be a minion. We say that a mapping
\xi\colon\mathscr{M}\to\Pol(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})

preserves minors up to
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-homotopy
if for all
n,m>0
,
f\in\mathscr{M}^{(n)}
, and
\pi\colon[n]\to[m]
, we have that
\xi(f^{\pi})
is
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-homotopic to
\xi(f)^{\pi}
.

We recall that the points in the geometric representation of
\mathsf{K}
can be viewed as convex combinations of vertices of
\mathsf{K}
, more precisely

\lvert\mathord{\mathsf{K}}\rvert=\{\sum_{v\in\sigma}\lambda_{v}v\mid\sigma\in\mathsf{K},\lambda_{v}\geq 0,\sum_{v\in\sigma}\lambda_{v}=1\}
and the representation of each point is unique (as we identified
v\in V(K)
with affinely independent vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{V(K)}
, namely the basis vectors).
This is used in the following lemma.

Lemma 3.21
.

Let
\mathsf{K}
,
\mathsf{K}^{\prime}
be two
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-simplicial complexes. Let
\mu_{2}\colon\Pol(\mathsf{K},\mathsf{K}^{\prime})\to\Pol(\lvert\mathord{\mathsf{K}}\rvert,\lvert\mathord{\mathsf{K}^{\prime}}\rvert)
be the linear extension, i.e.,
\mu_{2}
takes
f\in\Pol(\mathsf{K},\mathsf{K}^{\prime})
to

\mu_{2}(f)\colon(\sum_{v\in\sigma_{1}}\lambda_{1,v}v,\dots,\sum_{v\in\sigma_{n}}\lambda_{n,v}v)\mapsto\sum_{v_{1}\in\sigma_{1},\dots,v_{n}\in\sigma_{n}}\lambda_{1,v_{1}}\cdots\lambda_{n,v_{n}}f(v_{1},\dots v_{n})

for a point in
\Delta^{\sigma_{1}}\times\dots\times\Delta^{\sigma_{n}}\subseteq\lvert\mathord{\mathsf{K}}\rvert^{n}
.
Then
\mu_{2}
preserves minors up to
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-homotopy.

Proof 3.22
.

Let
f\colon\mathsf{K}^{n}\to\mathsf{K}^{\prime}
be a simplicial map and pick any
\pi\colon[n]\to[m]
.
Then

\displaystyle\mu_{2}(f^{\pi})(\sum_{v\in\sigma_{1}}\lambda_{1,v}v,\dots,\sum_{v\in\sigma_{m}}\lambda_{m,v}v)

\displaystyle=\sum_{v_{1}\in\sigma_{1},\dots,v_{m}\in\sigma_{m}}\lambda_{1,v_{1}}\cdots\lambda_{m,v_{m}}f^{\pi}(v_{1},\dots v_{m})

\displaystyle=\sum_{v_{1}\in\sigma_{1},\dots,v_{m}\in\sigma_{m}}\lambda_{1,v_{1}}\cdots\lambda_{m,v_{m}}f(v_{\pi(1)},\dots v_{\pi(n)}).

On the other hand, if we take the induced map first and then the minor, we obtain:

\mu_{2}(f)(\sum_{v\in\sigma_{\pi(1)}}\lambda_{\pi(1),v}v,\dots,\sum_{v\in\sigma_{\pi(n)}}\lambda_{\pi(n),v}v)\\
=\sum_{v_{1}\in\sigma_{\pi(1)},\dots,v_{n}\in\sigma_{\pi(n)}}\lambda_{\pi(1),v_{1}}\cdots\lambda_{\pi(n),v_{n}}f(v_{1},\dots v_{n}).

Both points lie in
\Delta^{\sigma}\subseteq\lvert\mathord{\mathsf{K}^{\prime}}\rvert
for
\sigma=\{f(v_{1},\dots,v_{n})\mid v_{i}\in\sigma_{\pi(i)}\}\in\mathsf{K}^{\prime}
. We can thus continuously move from one to the other.
Formally, we define a homotopy
h\colon\lvert\mathord{\mathsf{K}}\rvert^{m}\times[0,1]\to\lvert\mathord{\mathsf{K}^{\prime}}\rvert
by

\textstyle h(x_{1},\dots,x_{m},t)=t\mu_{2}(f)(x_{\pi(1)},\dots,x_{\pi(n)})+(1-t)\mu_{2}(f^{\pi})(x_{1},\dots,x_{m}).

It is clear that
h_{t}\colon\mathbf{x}\mapsto h(\mathbf{x},t)
is a well-defined
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-map, and therefore
h
is the required
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-homotopy.
(As a side note, observe that the homotopy is constant on vertices: for any vertices
v_{1},\dots,v_{m}
of
\mathsf{K}
and each
t\in[0,1]
,
h_{t}(v_{1},\dots,v_{m})
is equal to
f^{\pi}(v_{1},\dots,v_{m})=f(v_{\pi(1)},\dots,v_{\pi(n)})
.)

Lemma 3.23
.

Let
\mathbf{H}
be a non-bipartite graph. Then there is a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-map
r\colon\mathcal{S}^{1}\to\lvert\mathord{\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathord{\mathbf{H}})}\rvert
.

Proof 3.24
.

Since the graph
\mathbf{H}
is not bipartite, there is a homomorphism
h\colon\mathbf{C}_{k}\to\mathbf{H}
for some odd
k
. This induces a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-map
\lvert\mathord{h^{\prime}}\rvert\colon\lvert\mathord{\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathord{\mathbf{C}_{k}})}\rvert\to\lvert\mathord{\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathord{\mathbf{H}})}\rvert
, and since
\lvert\mathord{\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathord{\mathbf{C}_{k}})}\rvert
is
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-homeomorphic to
\mathcal{S}^{1}
(see Example
3.11
), the claim follows.

Lemma 3.25
.

Let
\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G}
be two graphs such that
\mathbf{H}
is non-bipartite,
\mathbf{H}\to\mathbf{G}
, and there is a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-map
s\colon\lvert\mathord{\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathord{\mathbf{G}})}\rvert\to\mathcal{S}^{1}
.
Then
\mu\colon\Pol({\mathbf{H}},{\mathbf{G}})\to\Pol(\mathcal{S}^{1},\mathcal{S}^{1})
defined as

\mu(f)(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}):=s(\mu_{2}\mu_{1}(f)(r(x_{1}),\dots,r(x_{n}))),

where
\mu_{1}
,
\mu_{2}
, and
r
are from Lemmas
3.18
,
3.21
, and
3.23
, respectively, preserves minors up to
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-homotopy.

Proof 3.26
.

Assume that
f
is a polymorphism from
\mathbf{H}
to
\mathbf{G}
of arity
n
and let
\pi\colon[n]\to[m]
. We want to prove that
\mu(f)^{\pi}
is
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-homotopic to
\mu(f^{\pi})
.
From Lemmas
3.18
and
3.21
, we have that
\mu_{2}\mu_{1}(f)^{\pi}
and
\mu_{2}\mu_{1}(f^{\pi})
are
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-homotopic; let
h^{\prime}
be a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-homotopy that witnesses this fact.
We define a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-homotopy
h\colon\mathcal{T}^{m}\times[0,1]\to\mathcal{S}^{1}
by

h(x_{1},\dots,x_{m},t)=sh^{\prime}(r(x_{1}),\dots,r(x_{m}),t).

Indeed, for
t=0
,

h(x_{1},\dots,x_{m},0)=s\left(\mu_{2}\mu_{1}(f)\left(r(x_{\pi(1)}),\dots,r(x_{\pi(n)})\right)\right)=\mu(f)^{\pi}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})

while for
t=1
,

h(x_{1},\dots,x_{m},1)=s\big(\mu_{2}\mu_{1}(f^{\pi})\big(r(x_{1}),\dots,r(x_{m})\big)\big)=\mu(f^{\pi})(x_{1},\dots,x_{m}).

This concludes the proof.

The final step is from
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-spaces to the fundamental groups. Recall that, for a continuous function
f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
, we have a group homomorphism
f_{*}\colon\pi_{1}(\mathord{\mathcal{X}})\to\pi_{1}(\mathord{\mathcal{Y}})
defined as
f_{*}([\ell])=[f\ell]
.
We will also need a group homomorphism from
\pi_{1}(\mathord{\mathcal{X}})^{n}
to
\pi_{1}(\mathord{\mathcal{X}^{n}})
, that is guaranteed to exist for any path connected space
\mathcal{X}
by
[
Hat01
, Proposition 1.12]
. One such homomorphism is the mapping
e_{n}\colon\pi_{1}(\mathord{\mathcal{X}})^{n}\to\pi_{1}(\mathord{\mathcal{X}^{n}})
defined as

e_{n}([\ell_{1}],\dots,[\ell_{n}]):=[t\mapsto(\ell_{1}(t),\dots,\ell_{n}(t))]

for
\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{n}\colon\mathcal{S}^{1}\to\mathcal{X}
.

Lemma 3.27
.

Let
\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}
be two path connected
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-spaces. Then the mapping

\nu(f):=f_{*}e_{n}

is a minion homomorphism from
\Pol(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
to
\Pol(\pi_{1}(\mathord{\mathcal{X}}),\pi_{1}(\mathord{\mathcal{Y}}))
. Moreover, if
f
and
g
are homotopic then
\nu(f)=\nu(g)
.

Proof 3.28
.

Assume that
f\in\Pol(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
is of arity
n
,
\pi\colon[n]\to[m]
, and
\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{m}\colon\mathcal{S}^{1}\to\mathcal{X}
.
To simplify notation, let
\ell(t)=(\ell_{1}(t),\dots,\ell_{m}(t))
go from
\mathcal{S}^{1}
to
\mathcal{X}^{n}
and
\ell_{\pi}(t)=(\ell_{\pi(1)}(t),\dots,\ell_{\pi(n)}(t))
go from
\mathcal{S}^{1}
to
\mathcal{X}^{m}
.
Using the definitions of
f_{*}
and
e_{n}
, we get

\nu(f^{\pi})(\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{m})=[f^{\pi}\ell]=[f\ell_{\pi}]=\nu(f)^{\pi}(\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{m}).

Finally, since
f_{*}=g_{*}
if
f
and
g
are homotopic, we also get that
\nu(f)=\nu(g)
.

We recall that
\pi_{1}(\mathord{\mathcal{S}^{1}})\simeq\mathbb{Z}
(Lemma
3.14
). In the following statement we identify the two isomorphic groups to obtain a minion homomorphism to
\Pol(\mathbb{Z},\mathbb{Z})
, the minion of all linear functions on
\mathbb{Z}
.

Corollary 3.29
.

Let
\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G}
be two graphs such that
\mathbf{H}
is non-bipartite,
\mathbf{H}\to\mathbf{G}
, and there is a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-map
s\colon\lvert\mathord{\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathord{\mathbf{G}})}\rvert\to\mathcal{S}^{1}
.
The mapping

\nu\mu

is a minion homomorphism from
\Pol(\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G})
to
\Pol(\mathbb{Z},\mathbb{Z})
assuming
\mu
is as in Lemma
3.25
and
\nu
is as in Lemma
3.27
.

Proof 3.30
.

Clearly,
\nu\mu\colon\Pol(\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G})\to\Pol(\mathbb{Z},\mathbb{Z})
is a well-defined mapping that preserves arities. We need to show that it also preserves minors. This follows from the facts that
\mu
preserves minors up to
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-homotopy (Lemma
3.25
) and that
\nu
is a minion homomorphism that is constant on
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-homotopy classes (Lemma
3.27
). More precisely, assume
f\in\Pol(\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G})
is of arity
n
and
\pi\colon[n]\to[m]
. Then
\mu(f^{\pi})
and
\mu(f)^{\pi}
are
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-homotopic, and therefore

\nu(\mu(f^{\pi}))=\nu(\mu(f)^{\pi})=\nu(\mu(f))^{\pi}

where the second equality follows from minor preservation by
\nu
.

We remark that, for any (
n
-ary) function
f\in\Pol(\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G})
, the coefficients of the linear function
\nu\mu(f)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}{c_{i}x_{i}}
can be naturally thought of as the degrees of
f
at the corresponding coordinates. Such degrees can be defined in a combinatorial way (see
[
KO19
]
) — the intuitions in that approach are still topological, but the technical proofs become somewhat ad-hoc.

3.4.2.
Bounding essential arity

To finish the analysis of polymorphisms from
\mathbf{H}
to
\mathbf{G}
necessary for applying Theorem
2.12
, we need to bound the essential arity of functions in the image of
\nu\mu
(defined above) and show that none of these functions is a constant function.
We achieve this by proving that the image of
\nu\mu
is contained in the minion
\mathscr{Z}_{\leq N}
for some
N
. Recall that this minion is defined to be the set of all functions
f\colon\mathbb{Z}^{n}\to\mathbb{Z}
of the form

f(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=c_{1}x_{1}+\dots+c_{n}x_{n}

for some
c_{1}
, …,
c_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}
with
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lvert c_{i}\rvert\leq N
and
\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{i}
odd.

The oddness of the sum of coefficients follows from a well-known fact about
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-maps on
\mathcal{S}^{1}
. We recall that
the degree
of a map
f\colon\mathcal{S}^{1}\to\mathcal{S}^{1}
is the integer
d_{f}
such that the induced map
f_{*}
on
\pi_{1}(\mathord{\mathcal{S}^{1}})=\mathbb{Z}
is
x\mapsto d_{f}\cdot x
.

Lemma 3.31

(
[
Hat01
, Proposition 2B.6]
)
.

The degree of any
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-map
f\colon\mathcal{S}^{1}\to\mathcal{S}^{1}
is odd.

Lemma 3.32
.

Let
\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G}
be two graphs such that
\mathbf{H}
is non-bipartite,
\mathbf{H}\to\mathbf{G}
, and there is a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-map
s\colon\lvert\mathord{\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathord{\mathbf{G}})}\rvert\to\mathcal{S}^{1}
. Let
\mu
be as in Lemma
3.25
,
\nu
as in Lemma
3.27
, and let
\mathscr{H}=\Pol(\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G})
.
If
f\in\Pol(\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G})
and

\nu\mu(f)\colon(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{i}x_{i},

then
\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{i}
is odd.

Proof 3.33
.

Consider the (unique) unary minor
h(x):=f(x,\dots,x)
of
f
. Since
\mu(h)
is a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-map, by Lemma
3.31
it has an odd degree, i.e.,
\nu\mu(h)\colon x\mapsto d_{h}x
for some odd
d_{h}
. Finally, since
\nu\mu
preserves minors, we get that
d_{h}=\sum_{i\in[n]}c_{i}
which we wanted to show to be odd.

The bound on the sum of absolute values of coefficients is given by the discrete structure of the involved graphs. The key here is that there are only finitely many polymorphisms of a fixed arity between two given finite graphs.

Lemma 3.34
.

Let
\mathscr{M}
be a minion on finite sets
A,B
. Assume that
\xi\colon\mathscr{M}\to\Pol(\mathbb{Z},\mathbb{Z})
is a minion homomorphism. Then there exists
N
such that for all
f\in\mathscr{M}
,
if
\xi(f)\colon(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{i}x_{i}
,
then
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lvert c_{i}\rvert\leq N
.

Proof 3.35
.

We first consider binary functions. There are only finitely many functions
f\in\mathscr{M}^{(2)}
, so clearly the sum of the absolute values of the coefficients of
\xi(f)
is bounded by some
N
. We argue that the same
N
provides a bound for all other arities as well. Let
f\in\mathscr{M}
and

\xi(f)(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=c_{1}x_{1}+\dots+c_{n}x_{n}
.
Let
\sigma\colon[n]\to\{0,1\}
be defined as

\sigma(i)=\begin{cases}0&\text{if $c_{i}\leq 0$, and}\\
1&\text{if $c_{i}>0$.}\end{cases}

I.e.,
\sigma^{-1}(1)
is the set of all coordinates of
\xi(f)
with positive coefficients, and
\sigma^{-1}(0)
of those with negative or zero coefficients. Now, let
g=f^{\sigma}
, that is,
g
is the minor of
f
defined by

g(x_{0},x_{1})=f(x_{\sigma(1)},\dots,x_{\sigma(n)}),

so
g
is obtained by identifying all variables of
f
that induce positive coefficients on
\xi(f)
, and also all those that induce negative coefficients. We let
\xi(g)(x_{0},x_{1})=c^{+}x_{1}+c^{-}x_{0}
. Since
\xi
preserves minors, we get that

c^{+}=\sum_{c_{i}>0}c_{i}
and
c^{-}=\sum_{c_{i}<0}c_{i}
. Note that
c^{+}\geq 0
and
c^{-}\leq 0
. Finally,

\sum_{i\in[n]}\lvert c_{i}\rvert=\sum_{c_{i}>0}c_{i}-\sum_{c_{i}<0}c_{i}=c^{+}-c^{-}=\lvert c^{+}\rvert+\lvert c^{-}\rvert\leq N

where the last inequality follows from the definition of
N
.

We can now finish the proof of Theorem
1.4
.

Proof 3.36

(Proof of Theorem
1.4
)
.

We assume that
\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G}
are two graphs such that
\mathbf{H}
is non-bipartite,
\mathbf{H}\to\mathbf{G}
, and there is a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-map
s\colon\lvert\mathord{\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathord{\mathbf{G}})}\rvert\to\mathcal{S}^{1}
. Let
\mu
be as in Lemma
3.25
,
\nu
as in Lemma
3.27
, and
N
be the bound obtained from Lemma
3.34
for
\mathscr{M}=\Pol(\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G})
and
\xi=\nu\mu
. We claim that
\nu\mu(f)\in\mathscr{Z}_{\leq N}
for each
f\in\Pol(\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G})
. Assume
\nu\mu(f)\colon(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{i}x_{i}
. We have that
\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{i}
is odd from Lemma
3.32
, and
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lvert c_{i}\rvert\leq N
by the choice of
N
. This concludes that there is a minion homomorphism from
\Pol(\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G})
to a minion of bounded essential arity, namely
\mathscr{Z}_{\leq N}
, and thus Theorem
1.4
follows from Theorem
2.12
.

3.5.
Proofs of Corollaries
1.5
and
1.6

To show that Theorem
1.4
implies Corollaries
1.5
and
1.6
, we need the following facts about the structure of
\lvert\mathord{\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathord{\mathbf{G}})}\rvert
for the relevant graphs
\mathbf{G}
. These facts seem to be folklore, but we include proofs for completeness.

0
1
2
4
5
3
6
(4,0)
(4,1)
(5,1)
(5,2)
(4,2)
(5,0)

Figure 5.
\mathbf{K}_{7/2}
with a complete bipartite subgraph (on the left) and the corresponding face of
\lvert\mathord{\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathord{\mathbf{K}_{7/2}})}\rvert
after mapping to
\mathbb{R}^{2}
(on the right).

Lemma 3.37
.

For any
2<p/q<4
and any square-free non-bipartite graph
\mathbf{G}
, there exist
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-maps

(1)

s_{1}\colon\lvert\mathord{\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathord{\mathbf{K}_{p/q}})}\rvert\to\mathcal{S}^{1}
, and

(2)

s_{2}\colon\lvert\mathord{\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathord{\mathbf{G}})}\rvert\to\mathcal{S}^{1}
.

Proof 3.38
.

(1)
We first define a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-map
g\colon\lvert\mathord{\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathord{\mathbf{K}_{p/q}})}\rvert\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
. We will show that the origin
0
is not in the image of
g
, which then implies that the map
x\mapsto g(x)/\lvert\mathord{g(x)}\rvert
is a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-map to
\mathcal{S}^{1}
.

First, we define
g
on vertices of the complex, i.e., oriented edges of
\mathbf{K}_{p/q}
: Place
p
points
x_{0},\dots,x_{p-1}
on
\mathcal{S}^{1}
in a regular
p
-gon pattern. Map the edge
(a,b)
to the point
x_{b}-x_{a}
. Then extend
g
linearly to the interior points of faces. Clearly,
g
is a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-map. See Fig.
5
for a visualisation of
g
for
\mathbf{K}_{7/2}
.

Let
\sigma
be a face. That is,
\sigma\subseteq A\times B\subseteq E(K_{p/q})
for some non-empty sets of vertices
A,B
.
For an edge
(a,b)
, the distance between
x_{a},x_{b}
on the circle (the length of the shorter arc between them) is at least
2\pi\cdot q/p
.
Since
p/q<4
, this is greater than
\pi/2
.
Hence there are no
a,a^{\prime}\in A,b,b^{\prime}\in B
such that
x_{a},x_{b},x_{a^{\prime}},x_{b^{\prime}}
occur in this order on the circle, as the distances would add up to more than
2\pi
.
Therefore, there is a line in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
that strictly separates
\{x_{a}\mid a\in A\}
from
\{x_{b}\mid b\in B\}
(indeed, scanning the circle clockwise, there is exactly one interval from
A
to
B
and exactly one from
B
to
A
, both of length at least
\pi/2
; any line crossing these intervals will do).
This implies that the convex hull of vectors
x_{a}-x_{b}
cannot contain 0 (since each such vector has a positive dot product with the normal vector of the line).

(2)
This statement follows from observing that
\lvert\mathord{\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathord{\mathbf{G}})}\rvert
is essentially 1-dimensional which loosely follows from the facts that there are no copies of the complete bipartite graph
\mathbf{K}_{2,2}
and that every free
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-space of dimension 1 maps to
\mathcal{S}^{1}
(see
[
Mat03
, Proposition 5.3.2(v)]
). We present a compressed version of the two arguments.

Let
E^{+}\cup E^{-}=V(\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathord{\mathbf{G}}))
be an arbitrary partition into two sets that are swapped by reversing the edges, i.e.,
-E^{+}=E^{-}
and
-E^{-}=E^{+}
. This means that we choose an orientation for each edge of
\mathbf{G}
, and denote by
E^{+}
the set of all edges of
\mathbf{G}
oriented the chosen way, while
E^{-}
is the set of all edges oriented the opposite way. We define a mapping
h\colon\lvert\mathord{\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathord{\mathbf{G}})}\rvert\to\mathcal{S}^{1}
on the vertices of
\lvert\mathord{\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathord{\mathbf{G}})}\rvert
by setting
h(e)=(1,0)
if
e\in E^{+}
and
h(e)=(-1,0)
if
e\in E^{-}
.

We extend this mapping to inner points of faces. First, observe that for every face
\sigma\in\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathord{\mathbf{G}})
with at least two elements, there is a vertex
u\in V(G)
such that either
\sigma\subseteq\{(u,v)\in E(G)\}
or
\sigma\subseteq\{(v,u)\in E(G)\}
, as otherwise we can find a copy of
\mathbf{C}_{4}
in
\mathbf{G}
.
We map
\lvert\mathord{\sigma}\rvert
for faces of the first form to the arc connecting
(1,0)
and
(-1,0)
with positive
y
coordinates, and
\lvert\mathord{\sigma}\rvert
for faces of the second form to the arc with negative
y
coordinates. More precisely, if
\sigma=\{(u,v_{1}),\dots,(u,v_{n})\}
, we let

a=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}\ (u,v_{i})

be a point of
\lvert\mathord{\sigma}\rvert
. Put

x=\sum_{(u,v_{i})\in E^{+}}\lambda_{i}-\sum_{(u,v_{i})\in E^{-}}\lambda_{i}

and
y=\sqrt{1-x^{2}}
(note that
\lvert\mathord{x}\rvert\leq 1
, so
y
is well-defined), and define
h(a)=(x,y)
.
Now to preserve the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action, we map the geometric representations of the faces of the second form to the arc with negative
y
coordinates analogously putting
y=-\sqrt{1-x^{2}}
. Clearly, the mapping
h
defined this way is continuous and it is easy to check that indeed
h(-a)=-h(a)
for each
a\in\lvert\mathord{\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathord{\mathbf{G}})}\rvert
.

4.
Adjunction

In this section we will use both graphs and digraphs, which by default are allowed to have loops.
We will work with certain (di)graph constructions that can be seen as functions from the set of all finite (di)graphs to itself. On one occasion in this section (Subsection
4.2.1
), we will allow the image of a finite digraph to be an infinite digraph; this will be specified. We denote the set of all finite graphs and digraphs by
Gr
and
Dgr
, respectively. The class of all (finite and infinite) digraphs is denoted by
\text{Dgr}_{\infty}
.

In this section, we explain what
adjunction
is and how it can be used to obtain reductions between PCSPs. The notion of adjointness we present is a special case of the more general notion of adjoint functors in category theory. We restrict our attention to an order-theoretic version thereof (i.e., to posetal or thin categories), which only considers the existence of homomorphisms; this is also known as a (monotone) Galois connection.
Generally, a monotone Galois connection between two preordered sets
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
is pair of maps

\lambda\colon P_{1}\rightarrow P_{2}
and
\gamma\colon P_{2}\rightarrow P_{1}
such that, for all
a\in P_{1}
and
b\in P_{2}
,

(4.1)

\lambda(a)\leq b\quad\text{if and only if}\quad a\leq\gamma(b).

For us, the preorder
\leq
will always be the homomorphism preorder
\rightarrow
, and the sets
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
will be either
Dgr
or
Gr
.
In this case,
\Lambda
and
\Gamma
are adjoint if, for all (di)graphs
\mathbf{H}
and
\mathbf{G}
, we have

(4.2)

\Lambda\mathbf{H}\to\mathbf{G}\quad\text{if and only if}\quad\mathbf{H}\to\Gamma\mathbf{G}.

In this case
\Lambda
is a
left adjoint
and
\Gamma
is a
right adjoint
. If, for some
\Lambda
, there exists such
\Gamma
we also say that
\Lambda

has (or admits) a right adjoint
. Similarly, we say that
\Gamma

has a left adjoint
if there exists such
\Lambda
.

Adjunction is an abstraction of a few concepts that are already present in the theory of (P)CSPs: notably, the
\Inv
-
\Pol
Galois correspondences of Geiger, Bodnarchuk, Kaluzhnin, Kotov, and Romov
[
Gei68
,
BKKR69a
,
BKKR69b
]
and Pippenger
[
Pip02
]
between sets of functions and sets of relations can also been seen as adjunctions where, in (
4.1
), the preorder on one side is the inclusion and the preorder on the other side is the inverse inclusion.
We remark that many constructions described in
[
BBKO21
, Sections 3 and 4]
(see e.g. Lemma 4.4 there)
form pairs of adjoint functions. We also remark that condition (
4.2
) makes perfect sense when
\Lambda
and
\Gamma
are maps between the sets of relational structures of different signatures (say, between the set of all finite digraphs and the set of all finite 3-uniform hypergraphs), and all results in Subsection
4.2
hold in this more general setting.

This section is organised as follows. In Subsection
4.1
, we show that the standard gadget reductions from the algebraic approach to the CSP can be seen as a special case of adjunction. In Subsection
4.2
, we give general results about adjunctions and reductions between PCSPs. In Subsection
4.3
, we apply specific cases of adjunction to prove our results about the hardness of approximate graph colouring and demonstrate that the reductions between PCSPs obtained there cannot be captured by the algebraic theory from
[
BKO19
,
BBKO21
]
. Finally, in Subsection
4.4
, we use another specific adjunction to prove that, in a precise technical sense, the complexity of promise graph homomorphism problem depends only on the topological properties of graphs.

To emphasise that many of our proofs in this section do not assume computability of reductions, we will use the following definition.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
\Lambda
be a function from
Dgr
to
Dgr
or from
Gr
to
Gr
.
We say that
\Lambda
is

•

a reduction
from
\PCSP(\mathbf{H}_{1},\mathbf{G}_{1})
to
\PCSP(\mathbf{H}_{2},\mathbf{G}_{2})
if it preserves the
yes
- and
no
-answers of the two problems, i.e., for any
\mathbf{I}
,
\mathbf{I}\to\mathbf{H}_{1}
implies
\Lambda\mathbf{I}\to\mathbf{H}_{2}
and
\mathbf{I}\not\to\mathbf{G}_{1}
implies
\Lambda\mathbf{I}\not\to\mathbf{G}_{2}
. Preserving
yes
-answers is also called
completeness
and preserving
no
-answers
soundness
;

•

log-space/polynomial-time computable
if there is a log-space/polynomial-time algorithm that on input
\mathbf{I}
outputs
\Lambda\mathbf{I}
;

4.1.
Adjunction in CSPs

The algebraic approach to the CSP studies certain constructions on templates of CSPs called
pp-powers
, and it asserts that if
\mathbf{A}
is a pp-power of
\mathbf{B}
then there is a log-space reduction from
\CSP(\mathbf{A})
to
\CSP(\mathbf{B})
. This reduction is a function from instances of
\CSP(\mathbf{A})
to
\CSP(\mathbf{B})
computable in log-space that we call a
gadget replacement
.
In fact, any such reduction is a left adjoint to the corresponding pp-power construction.
We present the notions simplified for digraphs and refer to
[
BKW17
, Section 3.1]
for more background. We will use the constructions from Example
1.1
as a running example in this subsection.

Both functions are parameterised by a
gadget
or a
primitive positive formula
(a
pp-formula
), thus giving a reduction for each gadget. A
digraph gadget formula
of arity
n
is a logical formula
\phi(x_{1},\dots,x_{n},y_{1},\dots,y_{n})
of the form

\exists{z_{1},\dots,z_{m}}\;.\;(u_{1},v_{1})\in E\ \wedge\ \dots\ \wedge\ (u_{k},v_{k})\in E\ \wedge\ (u^{\prime}_{1}=v^{\prime}_{1})\ \wedge\ \dots\ \wedge\ (u^{\prime}_{\ell}=v^{\prime}_{\ell}).

where
u_{i},v_{i},u^{\prime}_{i},v^{\prime}_{i}\in\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n},y_{1},\dots,y_{n},z_{1},\dots,z_{m}\}
. Such a formula can also be represented by a
gadget digraph

\mathbf{J}_{\phi}
, which is a digraph with distinguished vertices labelled
x_{1},\dots,x_{n},y_{1},\dots,y_{n}
, obtained from vertices
\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n},y_{1},\dots,y_{n},z_{1},\dots,z_{m}\}
and edges
\{(u_{i},v_{i})\mid i\in[k]\}
by identifying some of the vertices (according to the equalities in
\phi
).

If we want a gadget to transform an undirected graph to an undirected graph, we require that the gadget is
symmetric
, i.e., that the formula
\phi(x_{1},\dots,x_{n},y_{1},\dots,y_{n})
is logically equivalent to
\phi(y_{1},\dots,y_{n},x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
for all graphs, or that the gadget graph has an automorphism switching
x_{i}
and
y_{i}
for each
i
.

The subdivision from Example
1.1
is defined by the following digraph gadget formula of arity
1
:

\phi_{k}(x,y)=\exists z_{1},\dots,z_{k-1}\;.\;(x,z_{1})\in E\wedge(z_{1},z_{2})\in E\wedge\dots\wedge(z_{k-1},y)\in E

which is symmetric if we only consider undirected graphs. The corresponding gadget would be an (unoriented) path of length
k
connecting the two distinguished vertices
x
and
y
.

Definition 4.2
.

The
gadget replacement

\Lambda_{\phi}
assigned to a digraph gadget
\phi
is then defined by applying the following construction. Starting with a digraph
\mathbf{H}
,

(1)

for each vertex
v\in V(H)
, introduce new vertices
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}\in V(\Lambda_{\phi}H)
,

(2)

for each edge
(u,v)\in E(H)
, introduce a fresh copy of the gadget digraph
\mathbf{J}_{\phi}
while identifying
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
with
u_{1},\dots,u_{n}
and
y_{1},\dots,y_{n}
with
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}
; we denote the remaining vertices of this copy of
\mathbf{J}_{\phi}
by
z_{u,v}
for
z\in\{z_{1},\dots,z_{m}\}
.

Note that some of the vertices
u_{i},v_{j}
above might get identified, which can also result in long chains of identifications.
Nevertheless,
\Lambda_{\phi}
is log-space computable.
