Figure 6.
Example of gadget replacement and a pp-power.

The subdivision
\Lambda_{k}
of Example
1.1
is the same as the gadget replacement
\Lambda_{\phi_{k}}
. We show an example of application in Figure
6
.

Definition 4.3
.

The
pp-power

\Gamma_{\phi}\mathbf{G}
of a digraph
\mathbf{G}
defined by
\phi
is obtained by the following construction.

(1)

V(\Gamma_{\phi}G)=V(G)^{n}
, and

(2)

((u_{1},\dots,u_{n}),(v_{1},\dots,v_{n}))\in E(\Gamma_{\phi}G)
if
\phi(u_{1},\dots,u_{n},v_{1},\dots,v_{n})
is true in
\mathbf{G}
, in other words, there exists a homomorphism
e_{u,v}
from the gadget digraph
\mathbf{J}_{\phi}
to
\mathbf{G}
such that
e_{u,v}(x_{i})=u_{i}
and
e_{u,v}(y_{i})=v_{i}
for all
i
.

Again, it is not hard to check that
\Gamma_{k}\colon\text{Dgr}\to\text{Dgr}
from Example
1.1
is the same as the pp-power
\Gamma_{\phi_{k}}
, i.e., it is the graph on the same vertex set where two vertices are connected by an edge iff they are connected by a path of length
k
in the original graph. Again, see Figure
6
for an application.

The standard reductions used in the algebraic approach are of the form
\Lambda_{\phi}
: it is well-known (see
[
BKW17
, Theorem 13]
) that
\Lambda_{\phi}
is a reduction from
\CSP(\Gamma_{\phi}\mathbf{G})
to
\CSP(\mathbf{G})
for any digraph
\mathbf{G}
.
For example, it is not hard to see that
\Lambda_{\phi_{3}}
from our running example is really a reduction from
\CSP(\mathbf{K}_{3})
to
\CSP(\mathbf{C}_{9})
since
\mathbf{K}_{3}
is homomorphically equivalent to
\Gamma_{\phi_{3}}\mathbf{C}_{9}
, and hence it has the same CSP.
This observation also follows immediately from the fact that
\Lambda_{\phi}
and
\Gamma_{\phi}
are adjoint, which we show here directly.

Observation 4.4
.

Let
\phi
be a pp-formula. Then
\Lambda_{\phi}
and
\Gamma_{\phi}
are adjoint.

Proof 4.5
.

To prove that indeed
\Lambda_{\phi}
and
\Gamma_{\phi}
are adjoint, first assume
h\colon\mathbf{H}\to\Gamma_{\phi}\mathbf{G}
is a homomorphism.
Such a homomorphism is a map
h\colon V(H)\to V(G)^{n}
which can be seen as an
n
-tuple of mappings
h_{1},\dots,h_{n}\colon V(H)\to V(G)
. Further, since
h
preserves edges, we have that for each
(u,v)\in E(H)
,
\phi(h_{1}(u),\dots,h_{n}(u),h_{1}(v),\dots,h_{n}(v))
is true in
\mathbf{G}
, which gives a homomorphism
e_{u,v}\colon\mathbf{J}_{\phi}\to\mathbf{G}
such that
e_{u,v}(x_{i})=h_{i}(u)
and
e_{u,v}(y_{i})=h_{i}(v)
. We use these
e_{u,v}
’s to define a homomorphism
h^{\prime}\colon\Lambda_{\phi}\mathbf{H}\to\mathbf{G}
:

(1)

put
h^{\prime}(u_{i})=h_{i}(u)
for each
u\in\mathbf{H}
and
i
;

(2)

extend
h^{\prime}
to new vertices introduced by the second step of gadget replacement of the edge
(u,v)\in E(H)
by putting
h^{\prime}(z)=e_{u,v}(z_{u,v})
for all
z\in\{z_{1},\dots,z_{m}\}
.

Clearly,
h^{\prime}
is a homomorphism since each
e_{u,v}
is and there are no edges in
\Lambda_{\phi}\mathbf{H}
that are not included in some copy of
\mathbf{J}_{\phi}
.
For the other implication, assume
g\colon\Lambda_{\phi}\mathbf{H}\to\mathbf{G}
. We define
g^{\prime}\colon\mathbf{H}\to\Gamma_{\phi}\mathbf{G}
as

g^{\prime}(u)=(g(u_{1}),\dots,g(u_{n}))

for each
u\in V(H)
. It is straightforward to check that
g^{\prime}
is indeed a homomorphism. This concludes the proof.

One of the main strengths of the algebraic approach lies in a description of when such reductions apply, by means of polymorphisms and minion homomorphisms; see
[
BKW17
, Theorem 38]

(originally appeared in
[
BOP18
]
) and
[
BBKO21
, Theorem 4.12]
for the promise setting. We return to this later in this section (Example
4.13
).

4.2.
General results about adjunction for PCSPs

In the following lemma, we give a few basic and useful properties of adjoint functors that are well-known in category theory. We provide proofs of these facts for completeness.
We say that a function
\Lambda\colon\text{Dgr}\to\text{Dgr}
is
monotone
if
\Lambda\mathbf{H}\to\Lambda\mathbf{G}
for all
\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G}
such that
\mathbf{H}\to\mathbf{G}
; and it
preserves disjoint unions
if
\Lambda(\mathbf{H}_{1}+\mathbf{H}_{2})
and
\Lambda\mathbf{H}_{1}+\Lambda\mathbf{H}_{2}
are homomorphically equivalent for all digraphs
\mathbf{H}_{1},\mathbf{H}_{2}
(we denote disjoint union with
+
).

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
\Lambda,\Gamma\colon\text{Dgr}\to\text{Dgr}
be adjoint.
Then

(1)

\mathbf{G}\to\Gamma\Lambda\mathbf{G}
for all digraphs
\mathbf{G}
;

(2)

\Lambda\Gamma\mathbf{H}\to\mathbf{H}
for all digraphs
\mathbf{H}
;

(3)

both
\Lambda
and
\Gamma
are monotone; and

(4)

\Lambda
preserves disjoint unions.

Proof 4.7
.

We start by proving (1):
\Lambda\mathbf{G}\to\Lambda\mathbf{G}
implies that
\mathbf{G}\to\Gamma\Lambda\mathbf{G}
by adjunction.
Similarly for (2), observe that
\Gamma\mathbf{H}\to\Gamma\mathbf{H}
implies
\Lambda\Gamma\mathbf{H}\to\mathbf{H}
by adjunction.

For (3) assume
\mathbf{H}\to\mathbf{G}
. Then by (1), we have
\mathbf{H}\to\mathbf{G}\to\Gamma\Lambda\mathbf{G}
, and therefore by adjunction
\Lambda\mathbf{H}\to\Lambda\mathbf{G}
. This concludes that
\Lambda
is monotone.
Similarly from (2), we have
\Lambda\Gamma\mathbf{H}\to\mathbf{H}\to\mathbf{G}
and hence by adjunction
\Gamma\mathbf{H}\to\Gamma\mathbf{G}
, so
\Gamma
is monotone.

For (4), consider the disjoint union of digraphs
\mathbf{H}_{1},\mathbf{H}_{2}
.
Note that
\mathbf{H}_{i}\to\mathbf{H}_{1}+\mathbf{H}_{2}
for
i=1,2
implies
\Lambda\mathbf{H}_{1}+\Lambda\mathbf{H}_{2}\to\Lambda(\mathbf{H}_{1}+\mathbf{H}_{2})
by monotonicity.
To show the other direction, observe that
\mathbf{H}_{i}\to\Gamma\Lambda\mathbf{H}_{i}\to\Gamma(\Lambda\mathbf{H}_{1}+\Lambda\mathbf{H}_{2})
for
i=1,2
by (1) and monotonicity of
\Gamma
, hence
\mathbf{H}_{1}+\mathbf{H}_{2}\to\Gamma(\Lambda\mathbf{H}_{1}+\Lambda\mathbf{H}_{2})
and therefore
\Lambda(\mathbf{H}_{1}+\mathbf{H}_{2})\to\Lambda\mathbf{H}_{1}+\Lambda\mathbf{H}_{2}
by adjunction.

The next result is the main theorem of this subsection. It describes when an adjunction provides a reduction between two PCSPs. This theorem will be applied in the following two subsections to provide new reductions between promise digraph homomorphism problems of the sort that has not been described before.

Theorem 4.8
.

Let
\Lambda,\Gamma\colon\text{Dgr}\to\text{Dgr}
be adjoint.
Let
\mathbf{H}_{1},\mathbf{G}_{1},\mathbf{H}_{2},\mathbf{G}_{2}
be digraphs such that
\mathbf{H}_{i}\to\mathbf{G}_{i}
for
i=1,2
.
Then
\Lambda
is a reduction from
\PCSP(\mathbf{H}_{1},\mathbf{G}_{1})
to
\PCSP(\mathbf{H}_{2},\mathbf{G}_{2})
if and only if
\ \mathbf{H}_{1}\to\Gamma\mathbf{H}_{2}
and
\Gamma\mathbf{G}_{2}\to\mathbf{G}_{1}
.

Proof 4.9
.

Assume first that
\mathbf{H}_{1}\to\Gamma\mathbf{H}_{2}
and
\Gamma\mathbf{G}_{2}\to\mathbf{G}_{1}
. Then
\Lambda
preserves
yes
-instances because
\mathbf{I}\to\mathbf{H}_{1}
implies
\mathbf{I}\to\Gamma\mathbf{H}_{2}
(since
\mathbf{H}_{1}\to\Gamma\mathbf{H}_{2}
by assumption) and then
\Lambda\mathbf{I}\to\mathbf{H}_{2}
by adjointness. It also preserves
no
-instances because
\Lambda\mathbf{I}\to\mathbf{G}_{2}
implies
\mathbf{I}\to\Gamma\mathbf{G}_{2}
by adjointness and then
\mathbf{I}\to\mathbf{G}_{1}
because
\Gamma\mathbf{G}_{2}\to\mathbf{G}_{1}
(by assumption). Hence
\Lambda
is a reduction, as claimed.

For the converse, preserving
yes
-instances means that for
\mathbf{I}\in\text{Dgr}
,

\mathbf{I}\to\mathbf{H}_{1}
implies
\Lambda\mathbf{I}\to\mathbf{H}_{2}
. Using this with
\mathbf{I}=\mathbf{H}_{1}
, we get that

\Lambda\mathbf{H}_{1}\to\mathbf{H}_{2}
and thus
\mathbf{H}_{1}\to\Gamma\mathbf{H}_{2}
by adjointness.
Preserving
no
-instances means that
\Lambda\mathbf{I}\to\mathbf{G}_{2}
implies
\mathbf{I}\to\mathbf{G}_{1}
. Take
\mathbf{I}=\Gamma\mathbf{G}_{2}
. Since
\Lambda\Gamma\mathbf{G}_{2}\to\mathbf{G}_{2}
by Lemma
4.6
(2), we have
\Gamma\mathbf{G}_{2}\to\mathbf{G}_{1}
.

Naturally, we use the above theorem in the case that
\Lambda
is log-space computable to obtain a log-space reduction between the two PCSPs. In the same way, it can also be applied if
\Lambda
is polynomial-time computable, if the goal is to get a polynomial-time reduction, etc. Note that, in such applications,
\Gamma
need not be computable to guarantee the correctness of the reduction.

Remark 4.10
.

We note (a well-known categorical fact) that any two right adjoints
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
of
\Lambda
are homomorphically equivalent in the following sense: for all
\mathbf{G}
,
\Gamma_{1}\mathbf{G}
and
\Gamma_{2}\mathbf{G}
are homomorphically equivalent. This follows, for example, from the above theorem: we have that
\Lambda
is a reduction from
\PCSP(\Gamma_{1}\mathbf{G},\Gamma_{1}\mathbf{G})
to
\PCSP(\mathbf{G},\mathbf{G})
since
\Gamma_{1}
is a right adjoint to
\Lambda
, and consequently,
\Gamma_{1}\mathbf{G}\leftrightarrow\Gamma_{2}\mathbf{G}
since
\Gamma_{2}
is a right adjoint.

Corollary 4.11
.

Let
\Lambda,\Gamma\colon\text{Dgr}\to\text{Dgr}
be adjoint. Then

(1)

\Lambda
is a reduction from
\PCSP(\mathbf{H},\Gamma\mathbf{G})
to
\PCSP(\Lambda\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G})
, for all graphs
\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G}
such that
\mathbf{H}\to\Gamma\mathbf{G}
(or equivalently,
\Lambda\mathbf{H}\to\mathbf{G}
);

(2)

\Lambda
is a reduction from
\PCSP(\Gamma\mathbf{H},\Gamma\mathbf{G})
to
\PCSP(\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G})
, for all graphs
\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G}
such that
\mathbf{H}\to\mathbf{G}
.

Proof 4.12
.

For (1), the first condition of Theorem
4.8
is equivalent to
\mathbf{H}\to\Gamma\Lambda\mathbf{H}
, which holds by adjunction (see Lemma
4.6
(1)); the second condition is trivial:
\Gamma\mathbf{G}\to\Gamma\mathbf{G}
.
For (2), both conditions are trivial.

We remark that all reductions described in Theorem
4.8
can be deduced from the special case in Corollary
4.11
(1) by composing it with trivial reductions (that map every instance to itself).
Recall that there is a trivial reduction from
\PCSP(\mathbf{H}^{\prime},\mathbf{G}^{\prime})
to
\PCSP(\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G})
if (and only if)
\mathbf{H}^{\prime}\to\mathbf{H}
and
\mathbf{G}\to\mathbf{G}^{\prime}
; this is referred to as a
homomorphic relaxation

[
BBKO21
, Definition 4.6]
.
If digraphs
\mathbf{H}_{1},\mathbf{G}_{1},\mathbf{H}_{2},\mathbf{G}_{2}
satisfy the conditions of Theorem
4.8
,
then we have the following sequence of reductions:

\PCSP(\mathbf{H}_{1},\mathbf{G}_{1})\xrightarrow{\text{triv.}}\PCSP(\mathbf{H}_{1},\Gamma\mathbf{G}_{2})\xrightarrow{\text{Cor.~\ref{cor:adj1}(1)}}\PCSP(\Lambda\mathbf{H}_{1},\mathbf{G}_{2})\xrightarrow{\text{triv.}}\PCSP(\mathbf{H}_{2},\mathbf{G}_{2}).

Similarly, Corollary
4.11
(2) implies all reductions in Theorem
4.8
:

\PCSP(\mathbf{H}_{1},\mathbf{G}_{1})\xrightarrow{\text{triv.}}\PCSP(\Gamma\mathbf{H}_{2},\Gamma\mathbf{G}_{2})\xrightarrow{\text{Cor.~\ref{cor:adj1}(2)}}\PCSP(\mathbf{H}_{2},\mathbf{G}_{2}).

Example 4.13
.

What we described in Subsection
4.1
in the context of CSPs, can be generalised to PCSPs as follows.
The following are equivalent:

(1)

there is a pp-formula
\phi
such that
\Lambda_{\phi}
is a log-space reduction from
\PCSP(\mathbf{H}_{1},\mathbf{G}_{1})
to
\PCSP(\mathbf{H}_{2},\mathbf{G}_{2})
(i.e., there exists some gadget reduction between the two);

(2)

there is a pp-formula
\phi
such that
\mathbf{H}_{1}\to\Gamma_{\phi}\mathbf{H}_{2}
and
\Gamma_{\phi}\mathbf{G}_{2}\to\mathbf{G}_{1}
(i.e.,
(\mathbf{H}_{1},\mathbf{G}_{1})
is a homomorphic relaxation of a pp-power of
(\mathbf{H}_{2},\mathbf{G}_{2})
);

(3)

there is a minion homomorphism
\Pol(\mathbf{H}_{2},\mathbf{G}_{2})\to\Pol(\mathbf{H}_{1},\mathbf{G}_{1})
.

The equivalence of (2) and (3) is by
[
BBKO21
, Theorem 4.12]
. The equivalence of the last two items and (1) is implicit in
[
BBKO21
]
(see e.g. Lemma 4.11 there), but the equivalence of (1) and (2) follows directly from Theorem
4.8
above.

For example, all
NP
-hard (non-promise) CSPs are reducible to one another in this way. The understanding that one can get simple reductions between CSPs by relating their sets of polymorphisms goes back at least as far as
[
JCG97
]
. The use of pp-formulas and minion homomorphisms was initiated in
[
BJK05
]
and
[
BOP18
]
, respectively.

4.2.1.
Are all reductions given by adjunctions?

Theorem
4.8
raises a question whether all reductions between PCSPs are given by adjunctions, in the sense that every reduction is a left adjoint from some adjoint pair. By Lemma
4.6
(3–4), we have to restrict this question to reductions that are monotone and preserve disjoint unions.
We will show that the answer to this question is positive, with a small technical caveat that the right adjoint might produce infinite digraphs on a finite input. Note that this caveat is not an issue, since the right adjoint does not need to be computable.

This suggests that looking at classes of adjoints that generalise the simple gadget constructions
\Lambda_{\phi}
could lead to understanding an essential part of all reductions between PCSPs. In particular, we hope that the use of the PCP theorem in proving
NP
-hardness of PCSPs (see
[
BBKO21
, Section 5]
) can be superseded this way.
We remark that, e.g., the reduction in Dinur’s proof of the PCP theorem
[
Din07
]
is not necessarily monotone: this is due to the fact that the number of repetitions of the gap amplification depends on the size of the input.

The core of the argument in the proof of the following theorem is a well-known categorical statement (the adjoint functor theorem). Again, we provide a full proof for completeness.

Theorem 4.14
.

Let
\mathbf{H}_{1},\mathbf{G}_{1},\mathbf{H}_{2},\mathbf{G}_{2}
be finite digraphs such that
\mathbf{H}_{i}\to\mathbf{G}_{i}
for
i=1,2
, and let
\Lambda\colon\text{Dgr}\to\text{Dgr}
be a reduction from
\PCSP(\mathbf{H}_{1},\mathbf{G}_{1})
to
\PCSP(\mathbf{H}_{2},\mathbf{G}_{2})
. Assume additionally that
\Lambda
is monotone and preserves disjoint unions. Then there is a function
\Gamma\colon\text{Dgr}\to\text{Dgr}_{\infty}
with possibly infinite images such that, for all finite digraphs
\mathbf{H}
and
\mathbf{G}
, we have
\mathbf{H}\to\Gamma\mathbf{G}
if and only if
\Lambda\mathbf{H}\to\mathbf{G}
.
Moreover, we have
\mathbf{H}_{1}\to\Gamma\mathbf{H}_{2}
and
\Gamma\mathbf{G}_{2}\to\mathbf{G}_{1}
for any such
\Gamma
.

Proof 4.15
.

We define
\Gamma\mathbf{G}
to be the disjoint union of all finite digraphs
\mathbf{I}
such that
\Lambda\mathbf{I}\to\mathbf{G}
. Assuming that
\mathbf{H}
and
\mathbf{G}
are finite digraphs, we immediately get that
\Lambda\mathbf{H}\to\mathbf{G}
implies
\mathbf{H}\to\Gamma\mathbf{G}
. We first prove the other implication for connected
\mathbf{H}
: assuming that
\mathbf{H}\to\Gamma\mathbf{G}
, we get that
\mathbf{H}
maps to some connected component of
\Gamma\mathbf{G}
and thus
\mathbf{H}\to\mathbf{I}
for some finite
\mathbf{I}
such that
\Lambda\mathbf{I}\to\mathbf{G}
. This gives that
\Lambda\mathbf{H}\to\Lambda\mathbf{I}\to\mathbf{G}
since
\Lambda
is monotone. For disconnected
\mathbf{H}
, we use that
\Lambda
preserves disjoint unions, so we may repeat the above argument for each component separately.

The “moreover” claim is proved similarly to Theorem
4.8
. In particular, the proof that if
\Lambda
is a reduction then
\mathbf{H}_{1}\to\Gamma\mathbf{H}_{2}
is identical to the one in Theorem
4.8
. To prove that
\Gamma\mathbf{G}_{2}\to\mathbf{G}_{1}
, we cannot simply use preservation of
no
-instances on the possibly infinite
\Gamma\mathbf{G}_{2}
. Instead, we get that for every
\mathbf{I}
finite,
\mathbf{I}\to\Gamma\mathbf{G}_{2}
implies
\Lambda\mathbf{I}\to\mathbf{G}_{2}
, which implies
\mathbf{I}\to\mathbf{G}_{1}
(because
\Lambda
is a reduction). A homomorphism from the possibly infinite
\Gamma\mathbf{G}_{2}
to the finite
\mathbf{G}_{1}
is then given by compactness.

While monotonicity is a key assumption in Theorem
4.14
, preservation of disjoint unions can always be enforced on any reduction by first precomputing connected components of the input (which can be done in log-space due to
[
Rei08
]
), and then applying the original reduction on each of the components separately.

We note that all the proofs in this section reduce between decision problems; they can be adapted for search problems. For that we need to additionally assume that there is an efficient way to find a homomorphism
\mathbf{I}\to\Gamma\mathbf{G}
given a homomorphism
\Lambda\mathbf{I}\to\mathbf{G}
on input (note that
\mathbf{G}
is fixed here). All the adjoint pairs that we use in the following subsections indeed have this property.

From now on, we return to considering only finite digraphs.

4.2.2.
Reductions that have both a left and a right adjoint

In the two applications below, we use reductions that are a left adjoint from one adjoint pair and, at the same time, the right adjoint from another adjoint pair. (In fact, these reductions will be of the form a pp-power
\Gamma_{\phi}
, as described in Subsection
4.1
, for special gadgets
\phi
). The property of being both left and right adjoint has the following consequence.

Theorem 4.16
.

Let
\Gamma
be a log-space computable function that has a right adjoint
\Omega
, and a log-space computable left adjoint
\Lambda
.
Then
\PCSP(\Gamma\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G})
and
\PCSP(\mathbf{H},\Omega\mathbf{G})
are log-space equivalent for all digraphs
\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G}
such that
\Gamma\mathbf{H}\to\mathbf{G}
.

Proof 4.17
.

Corollary
4.11
(1) applied for
\Gamma
and
\Omega
gives that
\Gamma
is a reduction from
\PCSP(\mathbf{H},\Omega\mathbf{G})
to
\PCSP(\Gamma\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G})
. We claim that
\Lambda
is a reduction from
\PCSP(\Gamma\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G})
to
\PCSP(\mathbf{H},\Omega\mathbf{G})
. This follows from Theorem
4.8
applied to
\Lambda,\Gamma
: We need to check that
\Gamma\mathbf{H}\to\Gamma\mathbf{H}
, which holds trivially, and that
\Gamma\Omega\mathbf{G}\to\mathbf{G}
, which follows by Lemma
4.6
(2), since
\Gamma
and
\Omega
are adjoint.

4.3.
The arc digraph construction

Let
\mathbf{D}
be a digraph. The
arc digraph
(or
line digraph
) of
\mathbf{D}
, denoted
\delta\mathbf{D}
, is the digraph whose vertices are arcs (directed edges) of
\mathbf{D}
and whose arcs are pairs of the form
((u,v),(v,w))
.
In other words,
\delta\colon\text{Dgr}\to\text{Dgr}
is the pp-power
\Gamma_{\phi}
corresponding to (
n=2
and) the following gadget digraph:

x_{1}
x_{2}=y_{1}
y_{2}

or to the pp-formula
\phi=(x_{1},x_{2})\in E\wedge(y_{1},y_{2})\in E\wedge x_{2}=y_{1}
. It thus has a left adjoint
\delta_{L}=\Lambda_{\phi}
, though we will not need it. More surprisingly,
\delta
has a right adjoint
\delta_{R}\colon\text{Dgr}\to\text{Dgr}
.

Definition 4.18
.

For a digraph
\mathbf{D}
, let
\delta_{R}\mathbf{D}
be the digraph that has a vertex for each pair
S,T\subseteq V(D)
, where
S
or
T
can be empty, such that
S\times T\subseteq E(D)
, and an arc from
(S,T)
to
(S^{\prime},T^{\prime})
if and only if
T\cap S^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
.

We give a proof of the adjunction below for completeness.
While
\delta
will be the reduction we use,
\delta_{R}
will be useful for understanding the best reduction we can get from
\delta
.

Lemma 4.19

(
[
FT15
]
)
.

\delta
and
\delta_{R}
are adjoint.

Proof 4.20
.

Let
\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G}
be digraphs and let
h\colon\delta\mathbf{H}\to\mathbf{G}
be a homomorphism.
That is,
h(u,v)
is a vertex of
\mathbf{G}
for each arc
(u,v)
of
\mathbf{H}
,
and for every pair of arcs
(u,v),(v,w)
in
\mathbf{H}
,
there is an arc from
h(u,v)
to
h(v,w)
in
\mathbf{G}
.
We can define a homomorphism
\mathbf{H}\to\delta_{R}\mathbf{G}
as
v\mapsto\left(s(v),t(v)\right)
, where
s(v):=\{h(u,v)\mid(u,v)\in E(H)\}
and
t(v):=\{h(v,w)\mid(v,w)\in E(H)\}
.
Then
s(v)\times t(v)\subseteq E(G)
, so
(s(v),t(v))
is indeed a vertex of
\delta_{R}\mathbf{G}
.
Moreover, for every arc
(u,v)
of
\mathbf{H}
,
t(u)\cap s(v)
is non-empty, as it contains
(u,v)
; hence
(s,t)
is a homomorphism to
\delta_{R}\mathbf{G}
.

Conversely, let
(s,t)
define a homomorphism
\mathbf{H}\to\delta_{R}\mathbf{G}
.
That is,
s(v),t(v)
are subsets of
V(G)
such that
s(v)\times t(v)\subseteq E(G)
and for every arc
(u,v)
of
\mathbf{H}
,
t(u)\cap s(v)\neq\emptyset
.
We define a homomorphism
h\colon\delta\mathbf{H}\to\mathbf{G}
as follows:
choose
h(u,v)
to be an arbitrary vertex in
t(u)\cap s(v)
.
For any two arcs
(u,v),(v,w)
in
\mathbf{H}
, we have that
h(u,v)
is a vertex in
s(v)
and
h(v,w)
is a vertex in
t(v)
, hence
(h(u,v),h(v,w))
is an arc of
\mathbf{G}
.
Thus
h
is indeed a homomorphism
\delta\mathbf{H}\to\mathbf{G}
.

By Corollary
4.11
(2),
\delta
is a reduction from
\PCSP(\delta_{R}\mathbf{H},\delta_{R}\mathbf{G})
to
\PCSP(\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G})
, for all digraphs
\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G}
. Let us see what this gives for classical colourings, i.e., when
\mathbf{H}
and
\mathbf{G}
are cliques. Let us denote
b(n):={\binom{n}{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}}
.

Observation 4.21
.

For all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, there are homomorphisms

\mathbf{K}_{b(n)}\to\mathbf{\delta}_{R}\mathbf{K}_{n}\to\mathbf{K}_{2^{n}}.

Proof 4.22
.

Consider vertices of the form
(S,V(K_{n})\setminus S)
in
\delta_{R}\mathbf{K}_{n}
, for subsets
S
of
V(K_{n})
of size exactly
\lfloor n/2\rfloor
.
Clearly for any two such different
S,S^{\prime}
, the set
S^{\prime}
intersects
V(K_{n})\setminus S
, so these vertices from a clique of size
b(n)
in
\delta_{R}\mathbf{K}_{n}
.
For the other bound, note that mapping a vertex
(S,T)
to
(S,V(K_{n})\setminus S)
gives a homomorphism from
\mathbf{\delta}_{R}\mathbf{K}_{n}
to its subgraph of size at most
2^{n}
, and therefore to the clique
\mathbf{K}_{2^{n}}
.

In other words, if
\chi(\delta\mathbf{G})\leq n
(i.e., if
\delta\mathbf{G}\to\mathbf{K}_{n}
) then
\mathbf{G}\to\delta_{R}\mathbf{K}_{n}\to\mathbf{K}_{2^{n}}
, hence
\chi(\mathbf{G})\leq 2^{n}
.
Similarly, if
\chi(\mathbf{G})\leq b(n)
, then
\chi(\delta\mathbf{G})\leq n
.
Therefore,
\delta
has the remarkable property of decreasing the chromatic number roughly logarithmically (even though it is computable in log-space!). This was first proved by Harner and Entringer in
[
HE72
]
.

Observation
4.21
can be made tight
if we use another, somewhat trivial adjunction between digraphs and graphs:
Let
\sym\mathbf{D}
be the symmetric closure of a digraph
\mathbf{D}
and let
\sub\mathbf{D}
be the maximal symmetric subgraph of
\mathbf{D}
; so
\sub\mathbf{D}\to\mathbf{D}\to\sym\mathbf{D}
by the identity maps. Observe that
\sym
and
\sub
are adjoint:
\sym\mathbf{D}\to\mathbf{D}^{\prime}
if and only if
\mathbf{D}\to\sub\mathbf{D}^{\prime}
for all digraphs
\mathbf{D},\mathbf{D}^{\prime}
.
3
3

3

This is in fact the composition of two adjoint pairs: taking
\sym
and
\sub
as functions from digraphs to graphs and the inclusion function
\iota
from graphs to digraphs, we have
\sym\mathbf{D}\to\mathbf{G}
if and only if
\mathbf{D}\to\iota\mathbf{G}
and
\iota\mathbf{G}\to\mathbf{D}
if and only if
\mathbf{G}\to\sub\mathbf{D}
for all graphs
\mathbf{G}
and digraphs
\mathbf{D}
.

Composing the two adjunctions, we get that
\delta\sym
is adjoint to
\sub\delta_{R}
.
Therefore, for any digraphs
\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G}
with
\mathbf{H}\to\mathbf{G}
,
\delta\sym
is a reduction from
\PCSP(\sub\delta_{R}\mathbf{H},\sub\delta_{R}\mathbf{G})
to
\PCSP(\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G})
by Corollary
4.11
(2).
For cliques, Poljak and Rödl
[
PR81
]
showed the following.

Lemma 4.23

(
[
PR81
]
)
.

For all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\sub\delta_{R}\mathbf{K}_{n}
is homomorphically equivalent to
\mathbf{K}_{b(n)}
.

Proof 4.24
.

As before, mapping a vertex
(S,T)
of
\sub\delta_{R}\mathbf{K}_{n}
to
(S,V(K_{n})\setminus S)
gives a homomorphism to the subgraph induced by vertices of the form
(S,V(K_{n})\setminus S)
,
so we can restrict our attention to it.
A (bidirected) clique corresponds exactly to an antichain of sets
S
(in the subset lattice),
so by Sperner’s theorem (on the maximal size of such antichains) the largest clique has size
b(n)
.
Independent sets in this subgraph correspond exactly to chains of sets
S
,
thus by Dilworth’s theorem (on poset width) the subgraph can be covered with
b(n)
independent sets, giving a
b(n)
-colouring.

Lemma
4.23
is equivalent to the statement that for a undirected graph
\mathbf{G}
,
\delta\mathbf{G}=\delta\sym\mathbf{G}\to\mathbf{K}_{n}
if and only if
\mathbf{G}\to\mathbf{K}_{b(n)}
.
This, in particular, means that the chromatic number of
\delta\mathbf{G}
is determined by
\chi(\mathbf{G})
, namely
\chi(\delta\mathbf{G})=\min\{n\mid\chi(\mathbf{G})\leq b(n)\}
.
This together with Corollary
4.11
(2) implies that
\delta\sym
gives the following reduction for approximate colouring:

Lemma 4.25
.

\PCSP(\mathbf{K}_{b(k^{\prime})},\mathbf{K}_{c^{\prime}})
log-space reduces to
\PCSP(\mathbf{K}_{k},\mathbf{K}_{c})
, for all
k^{\prime}>1
,
c^{\prime}\geq b(k^{\prime})>1
,
c\geq k
such that
b(c)\leq c^{\prime}
and
k^{\prime}\leq k
.

Proof 4.26
.

By Theorem
4.8
,
\delta\sym
is a reduction between the two problems if
\mathbf{K}_{b(k^{\prime})}\to\sub\delta_{R}\mathbf{K}_{k}
and
\sub\delta_{R}\mathbf{K}_{c}\to\mathbf{K}_{c^{\prime}}
. Lemma
4.23
then implies that the first condition is satisfied if
b(k^{\prime})\leq b(k)
, which is implied by
k^{\prime}\leq k
, and the second is satisfied if
b(c)\leq c^{\prime}
.

Remark 4.27
.

We remark that the reduction in Lemma
4.25
cannot be obtained by using a standard gadget reduction captured by the algebraic approach
[
BBKO21
]
(see Example
4.13
).
In detail, since
b(4)=6
,
\PCSP(\mathbf{K}_{6},\mathbf{K}_{b(c)})
log-space reduces to
\PCSP(\mathbf{K}_{4},\mathbf{K}_{c})
for all
c\geq 4
. This contrasts with
[
BBKO21
, Proposition 10.3]
which says that there exists a
c
such that
\Pol(\mathbf{K}_{4},\mathbf{K}_{c})
admits no minion homomorphism to any
\Pol(\mathbf{K}_{k^{\prime}},\mathbf{K}_{c^{\prime}})
for
c^{\prime}\geq k^{\prime}>4
.
Therefore, constructions like
\delta
change the set of polymorphisms in an essential way and we believe that understanding the relation between
\Pol(\mathbf{H},\delta_{R}\mathbf{G})
and
\Pol(\delta\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G})
is an important question for future work.

4.3.1.
Proof of Theorem
1.9

One consequence we derive from Lemma
4.25
is a strengthening of Huang’s result:

Theorem 4.28

(Huang
[
Hua13
]
)
.

For all sufficiently large
k
and
c=2^{\Omega(k^{1/3})}
,
\PCSP(\mathbf{K}_{k},\mathbf{K}_{c})
is
NP
-hard.

We improve the asymptotics from a sub-exponential
c
to single-exponential: from
c=\Theta(2^{k^{1/3}})
to
c=b(k)-1=\Theta(2^{k}/\sqrt{k})
while at the same time relaxing the condition from “sufficiently large
k
” to
k\geq 4
.

See
1.9

This theorem is proved by starting from Theorem
4.28
and repeatedly using the reduction
\delta\sym
. Roughly speaking, each step improves the asymptotics a little. After a few steps, this results in a single-exponential function, and with slightly more precision, this results in exactly
b(k)-1
.
Moreover, one can notice that the requirements on “sufficiently large
k
” gets relaxed with every step. This allows us after sufficiently many steps to arrive at any
k\geq 4
.

We note it would
not
be sufficient to start from a quasi-polynomial
c=k^{\Theta(\log k)}
in Khot’s
[
Kho01
]
earlier result in place of Huang’s Theorem
4.28
.

Proof 4.29

(Proof of Theorem
1.9
)
.

We start with Theorem
4.28
which asserts a constant
C>0
such that

(4.3)

\PCSP(\mathbf{K}_{k},\mathbf{K}_{2^{\lfloor C\cdot k^{1/3}\rfloor}})\text{ is {NP}-hard, for sufficiently large $k$.}

After one reduction using Lemma
4.25
, we obtain the following.

Claim 1
.

\PCSP(\mathbf{K}_{k},\mathbf{K}_{\lfloor\mathord{2^{k/4}}\rfloor})
is
NP
-hard, for sufficiently large
k
.

Proof 4.30
.

Substituting
b(k)
for
k
in (
4.3
), we get that
\PCSP(\mathbf{K}_{b(k)},\mathbf{K}_{2^{\lfloor C\cdot b(k)^{1/3}\rfloor}})
is
NP
-hard, for sufficiently large
k
. We apply Lemma
4.25
; to show that it implies the claim, we need
b(\lfloor\mathord{2^{k/4}}\rfloor)\leq 2^{\lfloor\mathord{C\cdot b(k)^{1/3}}\rfloor}
. This follows since
b(m)\leq 2^{m}
for all
m
and
2^{k/4}\leq(2^{k}/k)^{1/3}\leq\lfloor\mathord{C\cdot b(k)^{1/3}}\rfloor
for sufficiently large
k
, and therefore

b(\lfloor\mathord{2^{k/4}}\rfloor)\leq 2^{\lfloor\mathord{2^{k/4}}\rfloor}\leq 2^{\lfloor\mathord{C\cdot b(k)^{1/3}}\rfloor}

as we wanted to show.

The second reduction gives:

Claim 2
.

\PCSP(\mathbf{K}_{k},\mathbf{K}_{\lfloor\mathord{2^{k}/4k}\rfloor})
is
NP
-hard, for sufficiently large
k
.

Proof 4.31
.

Substitute
b(k)
for
k
in the first claim to get that
\PCSP(\mathbf{K}_{b(k)},\mathbf{K}_{\lfloor\mathord{2^{b(k)/4}}\rfloor})
is
NP
-hard. Observe that

2^{k}/4k\leq b(k)/4

for sufficiently large
k
, and therefore

b(\lfloor\mathord{2^{k}/4k}\rfloor)\leq b(\lfloor\mathord{b(k)/4}\rfloor)\leq 2^{\lfloor\mathord{b(k)/4}\rfloor}\leq\lfloor\mathord{2^{b(k)/4}}\rfloor.

Hence by Lemma
4.25
,
\PCSP(\mathbf{K}_{b(k)},\mathbf{K}_{\lfloor\mathord{2^{b(k)/4}}\rfloor})

reduces to
\PCSP(\mathbf{K}_{k},\mathbf{K}_{\lfloor\mathord{2^{k}/4k}\rfloor})
.

After the third reduction, we get the following.

Claim 3
.

\PCSP(\mathbf{K}_{k},\mathbf{K}_{b(k-1)})
is
NP
-hard for sufficiently large
k
.

Proof 4.32
.

Again, substitute
b(k)
for
k
in the second claim to obtain that
\PCSP(\mathbf{K}_{b(k)}
,
\mathbf{K}_{\lfloor\mathord{2^{b(k)}/4b(k)}\rfloor})
is
NP
-hard.
Observe that
b(k)\geq\frac{3}{2}b(k-1)
for all
k\geq 1
, since

\displaystyle b(2k)

\displaystyle\textstyle=\binom{2k}{k}=\binom{2k-1}{k-1}\frac{2k}{k}=2\cdot b(2k-1)\geq\frac{3}{2}b(2k-1),\text{ and}

\displaystyle b(2k+1)

\displaystyle\textstyle=\binom{2k+1}{k}=\binom{2k}{k}\frac{2k+1}{k+1}=b(2k)(2-\frac{1}{k+1})\geq\frac{3}{2}b(2k).

Therefore,

\textstyle b(b(k-1))\leq b(\frac{2}{3}b(k))\leq 2^{\frac{2}{3}b(k)}\leq 2^{b(k)}/4b(k).

for sufficiently large
k
,
and consequently, by Lemma
4.25
,
\PCSP(\mathbf{K}_{b(k)},\mathbf{K}_{\lfloor\mathord{2^{b(k)}/4b(k)}\rfloor})
reduces to
\PCSP(\mathbf{K}_{k},\mathbf{K}_{b(k-1)})
.

Finally after the fourth reduction:

Claim 4
.

\PCSP(\mathbf{K}_{k},\mathbf{K}_{b(k)-1})
is
NP
-hard for sufficiently large
k
.

Proof 4.33
.

Substitute
b(k)
for
k
in the third claim to get that
\PCSP(\mathbf{K}_{b(k)},\mathbf{K}_{b(b(k)-1)})
is
NP
-hard.
By Lemma
4.25
this reduces to
\PCSP(\mathbf{K}_{k},\mathbf{K}_{b(k)-1})
.

This concludes the improvement in asymptotics.
To relax the requirements for
k
, we repeat the reduction enough times. Each step is given by the following claim.

Claim 5
.

\PCSP(\mathbf{K}_{b(k)},\mathbf{K}_{b(b(k))-1})
log-space reduces to

\PCSP(\mathbf{K}_{k},\mathbf{K}_{b(k)-1})
for all
k\geq 4
.

Proof 4.34
.

Lemma
4.25
gives the reduction since
b(k)
is strictly increasing and
b(k)>4
for
k\geq 4
, and hence
b(b(k)-1)\leq b(b(k))-1
.

To finish the proof, assume that
k^{\prime}\geq 4
and let
k_{0}
be sufficiently large so Claim
4
is true for all
k\geq k_{0}
. Since
k^{\prime}\geq 4
there is
n
such that
k_{0}\leq b^{(n)}(k^{\prime})
(where
b^{(n)}
denotes the
n
-fold composition of
b
). Applying Claim
5

n
times gives a log-space reduction from
\PCSP(\mathbf{K}_{b^{(n)}(k^{\prime})},\mathbf{K}_{b^{(n+1)}(k^{\prime})-1})
to
\PCSP(\mathbf{K}_{k^{\prime}},\mathbf{K}_{b(k^{\prime})-1})
, and since the first problem is
NP
-hard (Claim
4
) this concludes that
\PCSP(\mathbf{K}_{k^{\prime}},\mathbf{K}_{b(k^{\prime})-1})
is
NP
-hard for all
k^{\prime}\geq 4
.

4.3.2.
Proof of Theorem
1.8

In this section we prove a slightly more general result than Theorem
1.8
:

Theorem 4.35
.

If, for some loopless digraph
\mathbf{H}
and all loopless digraphs
\mathbf{G}
such that
\mathbf{H}\to\mathbf{G}
,
\PCSP(\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G})
is
NP
-hard, then
\PCSP(\mathbf{K}_{3},\mathbf{G})
is
NP
-hard for all loopless digraphs
\mathbf{G}
such that
\mathbf{K}_{3}\to\mathbf{G}
.

Indeed, it is easy to see that the assumption of Theorem
4.35
is slightly weaker than the assumption of Theorem
1.8
, while the conclusions of the two theorems are equivalent.

We prove Theorem
4.35
by iterating the reduction given by
\delta
in a similar way as in the last paragraph of the proof of Theorem
1.9
; in fact that part of the proof could be used with only minor changes to prove that
\PCSP(\mathbf{K}_{4},\mathbf{K}_{c})
is
NP
-hard for all
c\geq 4
if
\PCSP(\mathbf{K}_{k},\mathbf{K}_{c})
is
NP
-hard for some
k
and all
c\geq k
. We get to
\mathbf{K}_{3}
by omitting the intermediate use of
\sym
, i.e., we keep orientation of the edges. One step of the reduction is given by the following lemma.

Lemma 4.36
.

Let
\mathbf{H}
be a loopless digraph.
If, for all loopless digraphs
\mathbf{G}^{\prime}
such that
\mathbf{H}\to\mathbf{G}^{\prime}
,
\PCSP(\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G}^{\prime})
is
NP
-hard, then
\PCSP(\delta\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G})
is
NP
-hard for all loopless digraphs
\mathbf{G}
such that
\delta\mathbf{H}\to\mathbf{G}
.

Proof 4.37
.

Let
\mathbf{H}
be a digraph that satisfies the premise, and let
\mathbf{G}
be a loopless digraph such that
\delta\mathbf{H}\to\mathbf{G}
. We aim to prove that
\PCSP(\delta\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G})
is
NP
-hard.
Corollary
4.11
(1) gives a log-space reduction from
\PCSP(\mathbf{H},\delta_{R}\mathbf{G})
to
\PCSP(\delta\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G})
. We claim that the digraph
\delta_{R}\mathbf{G}
is loopless, which follows from the construction of
\delta_{R}
(see Definition
4.18
) and the assumption that
\mathbf{G}
is loopless: Indeed, if a vertex
(S,T)
in
\delta_{R}\mathbf{G}
has a loop, then
S\cap T\neq\emptyset
. Consequently,
\mathbf{G}
has a loop on any vertex
v\in S\cap T
since
S\times T\subseteq E(G)
. Clearly also
\mathbf{H}\to\delta_{R}\mathbf{G}
since
\delta
and
\delta_{R}
are adjoint. Therefore, we have that
\PCSP(\mathbf{H},\delta_{R}\mathbf{G})
is
NP
-hard by the assumption of the lemma, and we conclude that
\PCSP(\delta\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G})
is
NP
-hard as well.

To finish the proof of Theorem
4.35
, we will need the following two lemmas. The first one, which is a special case of the second, was independently discovered by Zhu
[
Zhu98
]
, Poljak
[
Pol91
]
, and Schmerl (unpublished, see
[
Tar08
]
). For the sake of completeness, we include the proof of Zhu
[
Zhu98
]
.

Lemma 4.38
.

There is a homomorphism
\delta(\delta\mathbf{K}_{4})\to\mathbf{K}_{3}
.

Proof 4.39
.

The vertices of
\delta(\delta\mathbf{K}_{4})
are two consecutive pairs of arcs, i.e., they correspond to triples
(i,j,k)
in
\{0,1,2,3\}
such that
i\neq j
and
j\neq k
. Two such triples
(i,j,k)
and
(i^{\prime},j^{\prime},k^{\prime})
are adjacent if
j=i^{\prime}
and
k=j^{\prime}
. We define
h\colon\delta(\delta\mathbf{K}_{4})\to\mathbf{K}_{3}
so that
h\colon(i,j,k)\mapsto j
if
j\in\{0,1,2\}
, and
h\colon(i,3,k)\mapsto c
for some
c\in\{0,1,2\}\setminus\{i,k\}
. It is straightforward to check that such
h
is a valid colouring.

We note that
\delta(\sym\delta\mathbf{K}_{4})
is
not
3-colourable, so it is important here to iterate
\delta
rather than
\sym\delta
.

The next lemma essentially shows that iterating
\delta
many times can bring a chromatic number of any finite loopless digraph down to
3
. We use
\delta^{(i)}\mathbf{D}
to denote the digraph obtained from
\mathbf{D}
by applying
\delta

i
times.

Lemma 4.40
.

For every loopless digraph
\mathbf{D}
there exists
i\geq 0
such that
\delta^{(i)}\mathbf{D}\to\mathbf{K}_{3}
.

Proof 4.41
.

If
\mathbf{D}
is a loopless digraph then
\mathbf{D}\to\mathbf{K}_{\lvert\mathord{V(D)}\rvert}
. Therefore, since
\delta
is monotone, it is enough to prove the statement for
\mathbf{D}=\mathbf{K}_{c}
and all
c>3
(the case
c\leq 3
is trivial).
Recall that
\mathbf{K}_{b(n)}\to\delta_{R}\mathbf{K}_{n}
for all
n\geq 1

(Observation
4.21
), and consequently
\delta\mathbf{K}_{b(n)}\to\mathbf{K}_{n}
. Recall that
b(n)
is strictly increasing. Now since
b(n)>4
for
n\geq 4
, there is
j
such that
b^{(j)}(4)\geq c
. Since
\mathbf{K}_{c}\to\mathbf{K}_{b^{(j)}(4)}
, the monotonicity of
\delta
implies
\delta\mathbf{K}_{c}\to\delta\mathbf{K}_{b^{(j)}(4)}
, which, with
\delta\mathbf{K}_{b^{(j)}(4)}\to\mathbf{K}_{b^{(j-1)}(4)}
, implies
\delta\mathbf{K}_{c}\to\mathbf{K}_{b^{(j-1)}(4)}
. Similarly, we get
\delta^{(2)}\mathbf{K}_{c}\to\delta\mathbf{K}_{b^{(j-1)}(4)}\to\mathbf{K}_{b^{(j-2)}(4)}
. Proceeding in the same way, we eventually get
\delta^{(j)}\mathbf{K}_{c}\to\mathbf{K}_{4}
. This together with Lemma
4.38
gives that
\delta^{(j+2)}\mathbf{K}_{c}\to\mathbf{K}_{3}
which gives the claim for
i=j+2
.

Proof 4.42

(Proof of Theorem
4.35
)
.

Assume that
\mathbf{H}
is a loopless digraph s.t.
\PCSP(\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G})
is
NP
-hard for all loopless digraphs
\mathbf{G}
with
\mathbf{H}\to\mathbf{G}
. Let
i
be such that
\delta^{(i)}\mathbf{H}\to\mathbf{K}_{3}
which exists from Lemma
4.40
. Now, iterating Lemma
4.36

i
times gives that
\PCSP(\delta^{(i)}\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G})
is
NP
-hard for all
\mathbf{G}
such that
\delta^{(i)}\mathbf{H}\to\mathbf{G}
. Since
\delta^{(i)}\mathbf{H}\to\mathbf{K}_{3}
,
\PCSP(\delta^{(i)}\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G})
trivially reduces to
\PCSP(\mathbf{K}_{3},\mathbf{G})
.

We remark that, if one iterates the reduction using
\delta
further, one cannot improve Theorem
4.35
to imply the full extent of Conjecture
1.2
. In particular, iterating
\delta
cannot be used to show
\PCSP(\mathbf{C}_{5},\mathbf{K}_{c})
is
NP
-hard for all
c\geq 3
given that
\PCSP(\mathbf{K}_{3},\mathbf{K}_{c})
is
NP
-hard for all
c\geq 3
. This is because
\delta^{(i)}\mathbf{K}_{3}
contains a directed cycle of length three, for all integers
i
.

Remark 4.43
.

Theorems
1.8
and
4.35
can be phrased in algebraic terms using so-called
\mathbf{H}
-loop conditions
, which recently gained popularity in universal algebra (see, e.g.
[
Olš19
]
) and featured in
[
BBKO21
, Section 6]
. Fix a digraph
\mathbf{H}
and let
(a_{1},b_{1}),\ldots,(a_{m},b_{m})
be the full list of arcs of
\mathbf{H}
.
The
\mathbf{H}
-loop condition is the following identity (i.e., function equation) involving two minors of a function
f

(4.4)

f(x_{a_{1}},\ldots,x_{a_{m}})=f(x_{b_{1}},\ldots,x_{b_{m}}).

(This definition is equivalent to the one used in
[
BBKO21
, Section 6]
).
One says that a minion
\mathscr{M}
satisfies the
\mathbf{H}
-loop condition if it contains a function
f
satisfying (
4.4
).

The following two statements are equivalent for each loopless digraph
\mathbf{H}
:

•

\PCSP(\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G})
is
NP
-hard for all loopless digraphs
\mathbf{G}
with
\mathbf{H}\to\mathbf{G}
; and

•

\PCSP(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
is
NP
-hard for all pairs of similar structures
\mathbf{A}
,
\mathbf{B}
such that
\Pol(\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B})
does not satisfy the
\mathbf{H}
-loop condition.

This claim can be proved in a similar way as
[
BBKO21
, Theorems 6.9 and 6.12]
.

In this interpretation, Lemma
4.36
can be rephrased as follows: If, for all PCSPs, the failure to satisfy the
\mathbf{H}
-loop condition implies
NP
-hardness, then so does the failure to satisfy the
\delta\mathbf{H}
-loop condition.
Can this perspective be used to bring some ideas from algebra (e.g.
[
Olš19
]
) to obtain better conditional hardness?

4.4.
Only topology matters

All graphs in this subsection are undirected.

Recall Example
1.1
. The functions
\Lambda_{k}
and
\Gamma_{k}
from this example are adjoint, for all
k
. More surprisingly, for odd
k
,
\Gamma_{k}
is itself the
left
adjoint of a certain function
\Omega_{k}\colon\text{Gr}\to\text{Gr}
, i.e., for all graphs
\mathbf{H}
and
\mathbf{G}
,
\Gamma_{k}\mathbf{H}\to\mathbf{G}
if and only if
\mathbf{H}\to\Omega_{k}\mathbf{G}
.
The graph
\Omega_{k}\mathbf{G}
for
k=2\ell+1
is defined as follows; the vertices of
\Omega_{k}\mathbf{G}
are tuples
(A_{0},\dots,A_{\ell})
of vertex subsets
A_{i}\subseteq V(G)
such that
A_{0}
contains exactly one vertex. Two such tuples
(A_{0},\dots,A_{\ell})
and
(B_{0},\dots,B_{\ell})
are adjacent if
A_{i}\subseteq B_{i+1}
,
B_{i}\subseteq A_{i+1}
for all
i=0\dots\ell-1
, and
A_{\ell}\times B_{\ell}\subseteq E(G)
.

If there is a homomorphism
f\colon\Gamma_{k}\mathbf{H}\to\mathbf{G}
, then a homomorphism
\mathbf{H}\to\Omega_{k}\mathbf{G}
is obtained by mapping
v
to
(f(N^{0}(v)),\dots,f(N^{\ell}(v)))
, where
N^{i}
denotes the set of vertices reachable via a walk of length exactly
i
.
Conversely, if there is a homomorphism
f\colon\mathbf{H}\to\Omega_{k}\mathbf{G}
, then a homomorphism
\Gamma_{k}\mathbf{H}\to\mathbf{G}
is obtained by mapping
v
to the unique vertex in the first set of
f(v)=(A_{0},\dots,A_{\ell})
.

We note that
\Lambda_{k}
and
\Gamma_{k}
are log-space computable, for all odd
k
; however,
\Omega_{k}
is not:
\Omega_{k}\mathbf{G}
is exponentially larger than
\mathbf{G}
.
Below, we will use the following observation that can be found together with more properties of the functions
\Lambda_{k},\Gamma_{k}
, and
\Omega_{k}
in
[
Wro19
, Lemma 2.3]
.

Lemma 4.44
.

For any graph
\mathbf{G}
and all odd
k
,
\Lambda_{k}\mathbf{G}\to\Omega_{k}\mathbf{G}
.

Proof 4.45
.

Since
\Gamma_{k}
and
\Omega_{k}
are adjoint, the claim is equivalent to
\Gamma_{k}\Lambda_{k}\mathbf{G}\to\mathbf{G}
(note that
\mathbf{G}\to\Gamma_{k}\Lambda_{k}\mathbf{G}
since
\Lambda_{k}
and
\Gamma_{k}
are adjoint, so we will prove that the two graphs are homomorphically equivalent). We describe one such homomorphism
h\colon\Gamma_{k}\Lambda_{k}\mathbf{G}\to\mathbf{G}
. Note that
V(\Gamma_{k}\Lambda_{k}G)=V(\Lambda_{k}G)\supseteq V(G)
. We put
h(v)=v
for each
v\in V(G)
and extend this to vertices introduced to
\Lambda_{k}\mathbf{G}
by replacing an edge
(u,v)\in E(G)
with a path of length
k
by alternatively mapping vertices on this path to
u
and
v
in such a way that
h
restricted to the path is a homomorphism from the odd path to
(u,v)
. It is straightforward to check that
h
maps any two vertices of
\Lambda_{k}\mathbf{G}
that are connected by a path of length
k
to an edge of
\mathbf{G}
, and therefore, it is a homomorphism from
\Gamma_{k}\Lambda_{k}\mathbf{G}
to
\mathbf{G}
.

The next lemma gives the key reduction for the main result of this subsection, Theorem
4.49
.

Lemma 4.46
.

Let
k
be odd and
\mathbf{G}
be a graph. If
\PCSP(\mathbf{H}^{\prime},\Omega_{k}\mathbf{G})
is
NP
-hard for all non-bipartite graphs
\mathbf{H}^{\prime}\to\Omega_{k}\mathbf{G}
, then
\PCSP(\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G})
is
NP
-hard for all non-bipartite graphs
\mathbf{H}\to\mathbf{G}
.

Proof 4.47
.

Let
\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G}
be non-bipartite with
\mathbf{H}\to\mathbf{G}
.
By Corollary
4.11
(2),
\Gamma_{k}
is a reduction from
\PCSP(\Omega_{k}\mathbf{H},\Omega_{k}\mathbf{G})
to
\PCSP(\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G})
.
To conclude that
\PCSP(\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G})
is
NP
-hard it remains to show that
\Omega_{k}\mathbf{H}
is non-bipartite. Observe that since
\mathbf{H}
is non-bipartite and
k
is odd then also
\Lambda_{k}\mathbf{H}
is non-bipartite. Furthermore, from the above lemma,
\Lambda_{k}\mathbf{H}\to\Omega_{k}\mathbf{H}
which implies that
\Omega_{k}\mathbf{H}
is also non-bipartite.

Thus (by the above lemma and homomorphic relaxation) if we know one graph
\mathbf{G}^{\prime}
such that
\PCSP(\mathbf{H}^{\prime},\mathbf{G}^{\prime})
is
NP
-hard for all non-bipartite
\mathbf{H}^{\prime}
, then we can conclude the same for all
\mathbf{G}
such that
\Omega_{k}\mathbf{G}\to\mathbf{G}^{\prime}
, for some odd
k
.
When does such a
k
exists? The answer, given in
[
Wro19
]
, turns out to be topological.
We remark that the results in
[
Wro19
]
use the so-called box complex of
\mathbf{G}
instead of
\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathord{\mathbf{G}})
. However, there exist
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-maps (in both directions) between the two complexes, see
[
MZ04
, Proposition 4(M2,M3,M7)]
for explicit maps. This is enough for our purposes, but a stronger claim is true — the two complexes are
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-homotopy equivalent (as defined in Appendix
A
)
[
Živ05
,
Cso08
]
.

Intuitively, while the operation
\Gamma_{k}
gives a “thicker” graph, the operation
\Omega_{k}
gives a “thinner” one. In fact,
\Omega_{k}
behaves similarly to barycentric subdivision in topology: it preserves the topology of a graph (formally,
\lvert\mathord{\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathord{\Omega_{k}\mathbf{G}})}\rvert
is
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-homotopy equivalent to
\lvert\mathord{\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathord{\mathbf{G}})}\rvert

[
Wro19
]
) but refines its geometry. With increasing
k
, this eventually allows to model any continuous map with a graph homomorphism; in particular we have the following.

Theorem 4.48

(
[
Wro19
]
)
.

There exists a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-map
\lvert\mathord{\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathord{\mathbf{H}})}\rvert\to_{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\lvert\mathord{\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathord{\mathbf{G}})}\rvert
if and only if
\Omega_{k}\mathbf{H}\to\mathbf{G}
for some odd
k
.

We now conclude the proof that whether the Brakensiek-Guruswami conjecture holds for a graph
\mathbf{G}
(and all relevant
\mathbf{H}
) depends only on the topology of
\mathbf{G}
— this was informally stated earlier in Theorem
1.7
).
In fact, it only matters which
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-maps
\lvert\mathord{\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathord{\mathbf{G}})}\rvert
admits.

Theorem 4.49
.

Let
\mathbf{G}
,
\mathbf{G}^{\prime}
be graphs such that
\lvert\mathord{\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathord{\mathbf{G}})}\rvert
admits a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-map to
\lvert\mathord{\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathord{\mathbf{G}^{\prime}})}\rvert
and suppose
\PCSP(\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G}^{\prime})
is
NP
-hard for all non-bipartite graphs
\mathbf{H}
such that
\mathbf{H}\to\mathbf{G}^{\prime}
. Then
\PCSP(\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G})
is
NP
-hard for all non-bipartite graphs
\mathbf{H}
such that
\mathbf{H}\to\mathbf{G}
.

Proof 4.50
.

By Theorem
4.48
,
\Omega_{k}\mathbf{G}\to\mathbf{G}^{\prime}
for some odd
k
. Since
\PCSP(\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G}^{\prime})
is
NP
-hard for all non-bipartite graphs
\mathbf{H}
, we also have that
\PCSP(\mathbf{H},\Omega_{k}\mathbf{G})
is
NP
-hard by a trivial reduction. Now, Lemma
4.46
gives the claim.

In particular, Theorem
4.49
implies that Theorems
1.3
and
1.4
are equivalent.

5.
Conclusion

We presented two new methodologies, based on topology and adjunction, to analyse the complexity of PCSPs and provided some applications
of these methodologies to considerably improve state-of-the-art in the complexity of approximate graph colouring and promise graph homomorphism problems.

As mentioned before, there are many ways in which topology can potentially be applied in the analysis of polymorphisms from
\mathbf{H}
to
\mathbf{G}
, for graphs or for general relational structures.
With the approach that we used, we made a few choices for our analysis. Specifically, we used (a) the graph
\mathbf{K}_{2}
to construct simplicial complexes
\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathord{\mathbf{H}})
and
\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathord{\mathbf{G}})
, (b)
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action on our complexes and topological spaces, and (c) fundamental groups of topological spaces.
One can try to perform similar analysis, but (a) replacing
\mathbf{K}_{2}
by any other graph
\mathbf{K}
(or, for general PCSPs, by another appropriate structure), (b) using any subgroup of the automorphism group of
\mathbf{K}
to account for symmetry (called “equivariance” in the topological literature) of the complexes and topological spaces, and (c) replacing the fundamental group with a different topological invariant of spaces or continuous functions involved.
Some examples of different choices, though not in the context of polymorphisms, can be found, e.g. in
[
Koz08
,
Mat03
]
. These are the obvious first choices of adapting the approach. Naturally, it can be changed in a more fundamental way: the most prominent seems to be directly analyse the topological structure of the simplicial complexes
\Hom(\mathbf{H}^{n},\mathbf{G})
(see
[
Koz08
, Section 9.2.4]
for related general suggestions). One advantage of this approach is that the analysis would depend only on the function minion
\Pol(\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G})
, rather than on the specific choice of
\mathbf{H}
and
\mathbf{G}
.

In this paper, we applied topology together with the algebraic theory from
[
BBKO21
]
to prove complexity results about promise graph homomorphism. However, our application can be seen as plugging the topological analysis into an algebraic result. Since topology appears to be naturally present in minions of polymorphisms, it would be interesting to further develop the algebraic theory from
[
BBKO21
]
to properly incorporate topology.
Similarly, we used adjunction to obtain some reductions for approximate graph colouring problems that are provably cannot be captured by the algebraic theory from
[
BBKO21
]
— it is natural to ask whether a more general theory can be constructed that incorporates both the current algebraic theory and adjunction.

It would be interesting to find further specific applications of our methodologies, for example, in approximate graph and hypergraph colouring and their variations, or possibly even beyond constraint satisfaction. Naturally, one would want to extend our methodologies to approximate graph colouring problems
\PCSP(\mathbf{K}_{3},\mathbf{K}_{c})
or promise graph homomorphism problems
\PCSP(\mathbf{C}_{k},\mathbf{K}_{c})
for
c\geq 4
. If one applies the same transformation of these graph problems into homomorphism complexes and topological spaces as we use in this paper, one would need to analyse the (
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-)polymorphisms from
\mathcal{S}^{1}
to
\mathcal{S}^{m}
for
m\geq 2
. (Note that
\pi_{1}(\mathcal{S}^{m})
is trivial for
m\geq 2
, so the fundamental group is of no use in this case).
These polymorphisms are
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-maps from tori
\mathcal{T}^{n}
,
n\geq 1
, to
\mathcal{S}^{m}
. This is somewhat related to some well-known hard open questions from algebraic topology, such as classification of maps from one sphere
\mathcal{S}^{m_{1}}
to another
\mathcal{S}^{m_{2}}
. However, to the best of our knowledge, the equivariant version of such questions was never considered. Moreover, for our purposes, it would suffice to get any classification of
\Pol(\mathcal{S}^{1},\mathcal{S}^{m})
that can be connected with the algebraic theory, e.g. with Theorem
2.12
above, or with
[
BBKO21
, Theorem 5.22]
or
[
BWŽ21
, Corollary 4.2]
. Of course, it is possible that some modification of our approach will need to be used. In any case, we believe that topology will play an important part in settling the complexity of approximate graph colouring and the Brakensiek-Guruswami conjecture.

Finally, we remark that the standard reductions from the algebraic approach, i.e., reductions of the form
\Lambda_{\phi}
(see Section
4.1
), can be thought of as replacing individual constraints in an instance with gadgets (possibly consisting of many constraints). Similarly, certain reductions of the form
\Gamma_{\phi}
, such as
\delta
and
\Gamma_{k}
presented in Sections
4.3
and
4.4
, can be thought of as replacing gadgets (i.e., certain parts of input) with individual constraints. The latter turned out to be particularly useful when they themselves admit some right adjoint
\Omega
(as was the case for
\delta
and
\Gamma_{k}
); however, such reductions must have a rather restricted form
[
FT15
, Theorem 2.5]
. Thus, a natural extension would be to investigate reductions which replace gadgets with gadgets (i.e., introduce a copy of one gadget for each homomorphism from another gadget).

Appendix A
Equivalence of homomorphism complexes

There is a superficial distinction between the abstract simplicial complex
\Hom(\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G})
as we defined it and the definition of the homomomorphism complex in
[
BK06
,
Koz08
]
. We will show that the two definitions give topological spaces that are equivalent in the following sense.

Definition A.1
.

Two
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-spaces
\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}
are
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-homotopy equivalent
if there are
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-maps
\alpha\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
and
\beta\colon\mathcal{Y}\to\mathcal{X}
such that
\beta\alpha
and
\alpha\beta
are
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-homotopic (recall Definition
3.15
) to the identity maps on
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
, respectively.

This notion is coarser than
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-homeomorphism (which required
fg
and
gf
to be equal to identity maps); for example,
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{(0,0)\}
is
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-homotopy equivalent to
\mathcal{S}^{1}
but not
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-homeomorphic to it.
Nevertheless,
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-homotopy equivalent spaces admit the same
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-maps and have isomorphic fundamental groups, for example, they are thus indeed equivalent for our purposes.

We remark, that in
[
Koz08
]
and other topological literature, very little attention is given to the distinction between abstract (simplicial) complexes and their geometric realisations. In particular, in
[
BK06
,
Koz08
]
, the Hom complex of graphs
\mathbf{H}
,
\mathbf{G}
is defined as a topological space and not an abstract simplicial complex (it is in fact a so-called
prodsimplicial complex
; see
[
Koz08
, p. 28]
). The following definition is an equivalent formulation of Definition 9.23 in
[
Koz08
]
using the comments thereafter.

Definition A.2
.

For a set
V
, we denote by
\Delta^{V}
the
standard simplex
with vertices
V
that is defined as a subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{V}
, where the canonical unit vectors are identified with elements of
V
, obtained as the convex hull of
V
, i.e.,

\Delta^{V}=\{\sum_{v\in V}\lambda_{v}v\mid\lambda_{v}\in[0,1]\text{ for each $v\in V$, and }\sum_{v\in V}\lambda_{v}=1\}
.

\Hom^{\text{prod}}(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathbf{G})
is a subspace of
\Delta^{V(G)}\times\Delta^{V(G)}
.
Thus a point of this space is described as a pair of formal sums
(\sum_{u\in V(G)}\lambda_{u}u,\sum_{v\in V(G)}\rho_{v}v)
such that
\sum_{u\in V(G)}\lambda_{u}=\sum_{v\in V(G)}\rho_{v}=1
.
Using this description,
\Hom^{\text{prod}}(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathbf{G})
is defined as the subspace consisting of those points
(\sum_{u\in V(G)}\lambda_{u}u,\sum_{v\in V(G)}\rho_{v}v)
such that

\{u\mid\lambda_{u}>0\}\times\{v\mid\rho_{v}>0\}\subseteq E(G)
is a complete bipartite graph.
The action of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
on this complex is given by switching the two coordinates, i.e.,

\textstyle-(\sum_{u\in V(G)}\lambda_{u}u,\sum_{v\in V(G)}\rho_{v}v)=(\sum_{v\in V(G)}\rho_{v}v,\sum_{u\in V(G)}\lambda_{u}u)
.

On the other hand, the geometric realisation of our
\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathbf{G})
can be described in the following way.
It consists of convex combinations
\sum_{(u,v)\in E(G)}\lambda_{u,v}(u,v)
(the points
(u,v)\in E(G)
are identified with certain unit vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
where
n=\lvert\mathord{E(G)}\rvert/2
so that
-(u,v)=(v,u)
) such that

\{u\mid\lambda_{u,v}>0\}\times\{v\mid\lambda_{u,v}>0\}\subseteq E(G)

is a complete bipartite subgraph.

Figure 7.
\Hom^{\text{prod}}(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathbf{K}_{4})

Both complexes are therefore defined using complete bipartite subgraphs, and the similarity is apparent. As an example that highlights the small differences, let us note that
\Hom^{\text{prod}}(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathbf{K}_{4})
is
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-homeomorphic to
\mathcal{S}^{2}
; the space is depicted in Fig.
7
. This is since, unlike in
\lvert\mathord{\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathord{\mathbf{K}_{4}})}\rvert
, the tetragonal faces on the picture correspond to actual squares in
\Hom^{\text{prod}}(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathbf{K}_{4})
.

Proposition A.3
.

Let
\mathbf{H}
,
\mathbf{G}
be graphs.
Then the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-spaces
\lvert\mathord{\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathbf{G})}\rvert
and
\Hom^{\text{prod}}(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathbf{G})
are
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-homotopy equivalent.

Proof A.4
.

We define continuous maps
\alpha
and
\beta
between the two spaces that witness the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-homotopy equivalence.

\begin{array}[]{rcccl}&\lvert\mathord{\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathbf{G})}\rvert&&\Hom^{\text{prod}}(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathbf{G})&\\
\alpha\colon&\sum_{(u,v)\in E(G)}\lambda_{(u,v)}(u,v)&\mapsto&\textstyle\left(\sum_{(u,v)\in E(G)}\lambda_{(u,v)}u,\sum_{(u,v)\in E(G)}\lambda_{(u,v)}v\right)&\\
&\sum_{(u,v)\in E(G)}\lambda_{u}\rho_{v}(u,v)&\mapsfrom&(\sum_{u\in V(G)}\lambda_{u}u,\sum_{v\in V(G)}\rho_{v}v)&{:\!\beta}\end{array}

It is straightforward to check that
\alpha
and
\beta
are
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-maps, that
\alpha\beta=\id
, and that
\beta\alpha
maps each point to a point in the same face. Thus, a homotopy from
\beta\alpha
to
\id
can be defined by linearly interpolating between the two:

(p,t)\mapsto(1-t)p+t\beta(\alpha(p))

for
p\in\lvert\mathord{\Hom(\mathbf{K}_{2},\mathbf{G})}\rvert
and
t\in[0,1]
.

We remark that the above proposition and its proof generalizes to arbitrary Hom complexes
\Hom(\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G})
with the action of
\Aut(\mathbf{H})
, more precisely, the complex
\Hom^{\text{prod}}(\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G})
(as defined in
[
Koz08
, Definition 9.23]
) is
\Aut(\mathbf{H})
-homotopy equivalent to
\lvert\mathord{\Hom(\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G})}\rvert
for any two graphs
\mathbf{H},\mathbf{G}
.
